Birational geometry of sextic del Pezzo surfaces
Elias Kurz, Egor Yasinsky

Abstract
. We study the biregular and birational geometry of degree 6 del Pezzo surfaces with Picard number 1, defined over an arbitrary perfect field. Using Galois cohomology techniques, we obtain an explicit description of cocycles for such surfaces and describe the Severi–Brauer varieties associated with them, recovering the biregular classification of sextic del Pezzo surfaces. We then compute the automorphism groups of such surfaces, describe their closed points in general position and investigate the structure of Sarkisov links at such points and the corresponding birational models, answering a question of M. Rost. Using this description, we show that degree 6 del Pezzo surfaces are the only solid surfaces that admit infinite pliability. We also find a system of generators and relations for the groups of birational transformations of such surfaces and use it to construct nontrivial quotients of these groups, including free groups on uncountable sets.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
12G05, 14E07, 14E05, 14J26, 14J50, 14E30, 14J45, 14M22, 20F05

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Biregular and birational classifications

The main goal of this paper is to study birational properties of del Pezzo surfaces of degree 6 defined over an arbitrary perfect field
\mathbf{k}
. As it is classically known, over
\overline{\mathbf{k}}
, every such surface is isomorphic to the blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
at three non-collinear points, and thus there is only one
\overline{\mathbf{k}}
-isomorphism class of such surfaces; furthermore, these are del Pezzo surfaces of the smallest anticanonical degree that possess a toric structure.

Despite their simple description over
\overline{\mathbf{k}}
, the biregular classification of sextic del Pezzo surfaces is much less trivial and, to the best of our knowledge, was first addressed in the unpublished note
[
Ros96
, Section 3]
of M. Rost and the work
[
CTKM08
]
of J.-L. Colliot-Thélène, N. Karpenko and A. Merkurjev as an auxiliary result for computing the canonical dimension of
\PGL_{6}
. A systematic treatment was later given by M. Blunk
[
Blu10
]
and M. Blunk, S. Sierra and S. Paul Smith
[
BSS11
]
, whose studies also included Quillen K-theory and derived categories of coherent sheaves for such surfaces. From the derived category perspective, sextic del Pezzo surfaces were further studied by A. Auel and M. Bernardara
[
AB18
, Section 9]
and A. Kuznetsov
[
Kuz21
]
. Models of del Pezzo fibrations of degree
\geqslant 6
via root stacks were investigated by A. Kresch and Yu. Tschinkel in a recent series of works
[
KT19
,
KT20
,
KT21
,
KT22
]
, while applications to rationality problems for cubic fourfolds was given by N. Addington, B. Hassett, Yu. Tschinkel and A. Várilly-Alvarado in
[
AHTVA19
]
.

In all these cases, the descriptions of sextic del Pezzo surfaces were obtained in rather algebraic terms, namely in terms of certain finite dimensional
\mathbf{k}
-algebras. This can be seen as a continuation of the classical algebraic descriptions of other toric del Pezzo surfaces. Recall that del Pezzo surfaces of degree 9, that is, Severi–Brauer surfaces, are in one-to-one correspondence with central simple
\mathbf{k}
-algebras of degree 3. Similarly,
\overline{\mathbf{k}}/\mathbf{k}
-forms of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, known as involution surfaces, are in bijection with central simple
\mathbf{k}
-algebras of degree 4 equipped with generalized orthogonal involutions, see
[
KMRT98
, §5B]
. The development of this viewpoint on toric varieties can be found in the works of A. Duncan
[
Dun16
]
, M. Ballard, A. Duncan, A. Lamarche and P. McFaddin
[
BDLM24
]
and also in a very recent preprint of A. Duncan and P. Singh
[
DS25
]
, where the authors developed a general framework for classifying torsors of algebraic tori in terms of Brauer groups.

Recall
[
CTKM08
, Proposition 5.1]
,
[
Lie17
, Definition 2.8]
that for any smooth projective geometrically irreducible variety
X
over
\mathbf{k}
, there exists an exact sequence of groups

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\Br(\mathbf{k})\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\Br(\mathbf{k}(X)).

The group
\Am(S)=\Pic(X_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}})^{\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{k}}/\mathbf{k})}/\Pic(X)\subset\Br(\mathbf{k})
is called the
Amitsur subgroup
of
X
in
\Br(\mathbf{k})
. The inspiration for our first result, Theorem
A
below, is the following characterization of certain twisted forms of quadrics, which uses the Amitsur group.

Theorem 1.1

(
[
Tre23
, Theorem 2.9]
)
.

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be two del Pezzo surfaces of degree 8 with
\Pic(S)\simeq\Pic(S^{\prime})\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. Then
S\simeq S^{\prime}
if and only if these surfaces have the same splitting field
\mathbf{F}
and
\Am(S_{\mathbf{F}})=\Am(S_{\mathbf{F}}^{\prime})
. In other words, the pair
(\mathbf{F},\Am(S_{\mathbf{F}}))
is a biregular invariant.

So, we begin by recovering the biregular classification of del Pezzo sextic surfaces focusing on the case of Picard rank 1 and using only minimal tools from Galois cohomology, namely an explicit description of cocycles; this will be useful in the remaining part of the paper. We formulate the resulting classification in terms of the Amitsur group, as was done in Theorem
1.1
from above.

Theorem A

(cf.
[
CTKM08
, Theorem 4.2]
,
[
Blu10
, Theorem 3.4]
)
.

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be two del Pezzo surfaces of degree 6 over a perfect field
\mathbf{k}
, with
\Pic(S)\simeq\Pic(S^{\prime})\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\mathbf{F}
and
\mathbf{F}^{\prime}
be the minimal extensions of
\mathbf{k}
over which all
(-1)
-curves on
S
and
S^{\prime}
, respectively, are defined. Then
S\simeq S^{\prime}
if and only if the following holds.

(1)

\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}^{\prime}
.

(2)

\Am(S_{\mathbf{K}})=\Am(S^{\prime}_{\mathbf{K}})
, where
\mathbf{K}
is the unique quadratic extension
\mathbf{k}\subset\mathbf{K}\subset\mathbf{F}
such that
\Pic(S_{\mathbf{K}})\simeq\Pic(S_{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime})\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

(3)

\Am(S_{\mathbf{L}})=\Am(S^{\prime}_{\mathbf{L}})
, where
\mathbf{L}
is the fixed subfield of the unique non-trivial central element of
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})
, whenever such element exists (if it does not, the condition (3) is omitted).

The starting point of our approach is essentially the same as in all the aforementioned works: namely, a pair of field extensions of the base field over which certain birational contractions on a higher-degree del Pezzo surface are defined. However, we then focus primarily on this geometric data, which we refer to as
Severi–Brauer data
. So, in more geometric terms and less formally, Theorem
A
means that two del Pezzo surfaces of degree 6 are isomorphic if and only if they have equivalent Severi–Brauer data, see Theorem
3.10
,
3.14
and
3.19
for more precise statements.

Our perspective is birational and situated within the paradigm of the Minimal Model Program, aiming to describe birational transformations of such surfaces and decide when two del Pezzo surfaces of degree 6 are birational, answering in particular
[
Ros96
, Question 2]
. The birational geometry of
non-trivial
Severi–Brauer surfaces is rather poor: if two such surfaces are birational, then they are either isomorphic or opposite (that is, they correspond to opposite central simple algebras in the Brauer group
\Br(\mathbf{k})
), see
[
Roq63
, Theorem 5]
,
[
Wei89
]
, or
[
Wei22
, Lemma 1]
. Surprisingly, the birational classification of del Pezzo surfaces of degree 8 was achieved only quite recently by A. Trepalin
[
Tre23
]
and J.-L. Colliot-Thélène
[
CT21
]
, with an input from J. Kollár’s work
[
Kol05
, 7]
. If
S
is a del Pezzo surface of degree 8 with
S(\mathbf{k})=\varnothing
and
\Pic(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
, then
[
Tre23
, Theorem 1.6]
states that any minimal surface birationally equivalent to
S
is isomorphic to
S
. In particular, the pair
(\mathbf{F},\Am(S_{\mathbf{F}}))
from Theorem
1.1
is not only a biregular but also a
birational invariant
. Our next theorem shows that the birational classification of del Pezzo surfaces of degree 6 is more subtle; we will also see that the natural field extensions associated with such a surface are, in general, not birational invariants.

We use the notation of Theorem
A
.

Theorem B
.

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be two del Pezzo surfaces of degree
6
over a perfect field
\mathbf{k}
, with
\Pic(S)\simeq\Pic(S^{\prime})\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. Then
S
and
S^{\prime}
are birational if and only if one of the following holds:

(1)

\Am(S_{\mathbf{K}})=\Am(S^{\prime}_{\mathbf{K}^{\prime}})=\Am(S_{\mathbf{L}})=\Am(S^{\prime}_{\mathbf{L}^{\prime}})=0
.

(2)

\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
,
\Am(S_{\mathbf{L}})=\Am(S^{\prime}_{\mathbf{L}})\neq 0
,
\Am(S_{\mathbf{K}})=\Am(S^{\prime}_{\mathbf{K}^{\prime}})=0
, and
S
has a point of degree 2 whose splitting field is
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
.

(3)

\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
,
\Am(S_{\mathbf{K}})=\Am(S^{\prime}_{\mathbf{K}})\neq 0
,
\Am(S_{\mathbf{L}})=\Am(S^{\prime}_{\mathbf{L}^{\prime}})=0
, and
S
has a point of degree 3 whose splitting field is
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}

(4)

\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
,
\Am(S_{\mathbf{K}})=\Am(S^{\prime}_{\mathbf{K}})\neq 0
, and
\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
,
\Am(S_{\mathbf{L}})=\Am(S^{\prime}_{\mathbf{L}})\neq 0
.

1.2.
Pliability of del Pezzo surfaces

The study of birational models of Mori fibre spaces and, in particular, of Fano varieties is a vast and one of the most important topics in modern birational geometry. One of the key concepts in this theory is the notion of
birational rigidity
. It originates in the famous work of V. Iskovskikh and Yu. Manin who showed that a complex smooth quartic 3-fold has no birational maps to Fano varieties, other
than isomorphisms to itself. In this paper, we study birational maps between geometrically rational 2-dimensional Mori fibre spaces. These are either del Pezzo surfaces
S
with
\Pic(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
, considered as fibrations over
B=\Spec\mathbf{k}
, or conic bundles
\pi\colon S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,B
with
\Pic(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
B_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}^{1}
.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
S
be a del Pezzo surface over
\mathbf{k}
with
\Pic(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. It is called
birationally rigid
if for any birational map
S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S^{\prime}
to the total space
S^{\prime}
of another Mori fibre space
S^{\prime}\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,B^{\prime}
one has
S\simeq S^{\prime}
. If, moreover, every birational self-map
S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S
is actually biregular, then
S
is called
birationally superrigid
.

Finally, if
S
is not birational to (the total space of) any conic bundle
S^{\prime}
, then
S
is called
solid
.

Following A. Corti
[
CM04
,
CR00
]
, one can argue that the notion of birational rigidity is too restrictive and one should study varieties which are birational to only
finitely many
different Mori fibre spaces, up to some natural equivalence. Let
\pi\colon S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,B
and
\pi^{\prime}\colon T^{\prime}\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,B^{\prime}
be two such Mori fibre spaces of dimension 2. If there is a birational map
\varphi\colon S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S^{\prime}
fitting into the commutative diagram

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}}

where
\sigma
is an isomorphism, and we have an induced isomorphism of generic fibres, then we say that our Mori fibre spaces are
square equivalent
.

Definition 1.3

(cf.
[
CM04
, Definition 1.3]
)
.

The
pliability
of a Mori fibre space
S/B
is the set (or, in some references, the cardinality of the set)

\Pl(S/B)=\{S^{\prime}\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,B^{\prime}\ \text{is a Mori fibre space}\ |\ \text{$S^{\prime}$ is birational to $S$}\}\ /\ \text{square equivalence}.

So, a solid del Pezzo surface
S
is birationally rigid if and only if
\Pl(S)=1
. If we know that our del Pezzo surface cannot be birationally transformed into fibrations with a base of positive dimension, then it is natural to ask into which (and how many) other minimal del Pezzo surfaces it can be transformed, and more generally — whether the number of such birational models is finite. Our Theorem
C
provides an answer to this question in dimension 2, and also shows that del Pezzo surfaces of degree 6 possess, in a certain sense, an exceptional property that sets them apart from all other del Pezzo surfaces.

Let
\indexx(S)
be the greatest common divisor of closed points on
S
.

Theorem C
.

Let
S
be a birationally solid del Pezzo surface over
\mathbf{k}
. Suppose that
\Pl(S)=\infty
. Then
S
is a sextic del Pezzo surface with
\indexx(S)=2
or
\indexx(S)=3
. Furthermore, there exist a field
\mathbf{k}
and a solid sextic del Pezzo surface
S
over
\mathbf{k}
such that
\Pl(S)=\infty
.

Remark 1.4
.

In general, it is not obvious how to construct irrational Fano varieties of Picard number 1 with large pliability, including the equivariant setting of so-called
G
-Fano varieties, see e.g.
[
CS23
,
CS16
,
Yas25
]
The pioneering work of A. Corti and M. Mella
[
CM04
]
provides an example of a singular quartic threefold
X
with
\Pl(X)=2
, the other model being a Fano threefold. The work
[
Sar23
]
of A. Sarikyan gives an example of a Fano–Enriques threefold with pliability 9 (however, these are not solid, as they admit del Pezzo fibrations as models). As far as we know, Theorem
C
provides the first example in the literature of a solid Fano variety with infinite pliability.

In Theorem
6.13
, we will formulate an explicit criterion for birational rigidity. A surface with infinite pliability will be constructed in Example
5.16
.

1.3.
Groups of birational self-maps

There is an extensive amount of literature on groups of birational transformations of the projective plane
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{k}}^{2}
over
\mathbf{k}
, known as the plane Cremona group
\Bir(\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{k}}^{2})
; here we do not attempt to survey these results and refer the reader elsewhere. All known presentations of these groups are quite involved and probably cannot be significantly simplified, since the projective plane typically admits many different birational transformations into two-dimensional Mori fibrations (see Section
2.5
on the Sarkisov program). By contrast, as was noticed above, a non-trivial Severi-Brauer surface
S
admits only two birational models: one has
\Pl(S)=2
. Despite this unpromising property, the group of its birational transformations
\Bir(S)
enjoys some peculiar features
[
Shr20
,
Shr21
,
BSY22
,
Kur25
]
. For example, in
[
BSY22
, Theorem D]
, it was shown that it admits a surjective homomorphism

\Bir(S)\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.63647pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\bigoplus_{I}\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}\ast\left(\bigast_{J}\mathbb{Z}\right),

where
|I|\geqslant 1
, and both
I
and
J
can be infinite and even uncountable in certain cases. In particular, unlike plane Cremona groups, they are not generated by involutions and, typically, by elements of finite order; (The generation of plane Cremona groups by involutions was shown in
[
LS24
]
.) This is the first known example of del Pezzo surfaces enjoying such property.

In this paper, we show that a similar property holds for certain del Pezzo surfaces of degree 6.

Theorem D
.

Let
S
be a del Pezzo surface of degree 6 over a perfect field
\mathbf{k}
with
\rk\Pic(S)=1
and
S(\mathbf{k})=\varnothing
. Then one and only one of the following holds.

(1)

If
\indexx(S)=2
, then there is a non-trivial surjective group homomorphism

\Bir_{\mathbf{k}}(S)\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.63647pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\left(\bigoplus_{I}\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\right)\ast\left(\bigast_{J}\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\right),

where
I\neq\varnothing
.

(2)

If
\indexx(S)=3
, then there is a non-trivial surjective group homomorphism

\Bir_{\mathbf{k}}(S)\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.63647pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\left(\bigast_{I}\mathbb{Z}\right)\ast\left(\bigast_{J}\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\right),

where at least one of the sets
I
and
J
is not empty. In particular,
\Bir_{\mathbf{k}}(S)
is not generated by elements of finite order, once
I\neq\varnothing
. The latter condition is satisfied for every
S
having
\Pl(S)\geqslant 3
.

(3)

If
\indexx(S)=6
, then
\Bir_{\mathbf{k}}(S)=\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S)
is an abelian group.

Roughly speaking, the sets
I
in the homomorphisms above correspond to closed points on
S
that allow transitions to other birational models via Sarkisov links at those points, while the sets
J
correspond to points where Sarkisov links lead to an isomorphic surface. For precise definitions, we refer the reader to Section
7
.

This paper is organized as follows. In Section
1
, we recall the language of Galois cohomology, as well as the basic properties of Severi–Brauer surfaces (including some explicit computations of twisted actions on them, which we were unable to find in the literature), and also the birational classification of del Pezzo surfaces of degree 8. We then briefly review the Sarkisov theory. Section
3
is devoted to the classification of non-
\mathbf{k}
-rational sextic del Pezzo surfaces of Picard rank 1 up to isomorphism, using an explicit description of cocycles, and so we prove Theorem
A
. In Section
4
, we compute the automorphism groups of such surfaces. In Section
5
, we describe closed points of degrees 2 and 3 on our surfaces and study the generality of their positions. In Section
6
, we study Sarkisov links between del Pezzo surfaces of degree 6 and, as a consequence, derive Theorem
C
. Section
7
opens with the proof of Theorem
B
. The remaining part is devoted to the proof of Theorem
D
. Along the way, we give the presentation of the groups
\Bir_{\mathbf{k}}(S)
, where
S
is a minimal sextic del Pezzo surface of index 2 or 3.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Fields and Galois theory

In this paper, we will mostly work with finite Galois extensions of our base field
\mathbf{k}
, which is assumed to be perfect. For the convenience of the reader, below we recollect some basic algebraic facts which will be systematically used throughout this text. Their proofs can be found in
[
DF04
, 14.4]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\mathbf{E}_{1}
and
\mathbf{E}_{2}
be two Galois extensions of a field
\mathbf{k}
. Fix the algebraic closure
\overline{\mathbf{k}}
of
\mathbf{k}
. Then the intersection
\mathbf{E}_{1}\cap\mathbf{E}_{2}
in
\overline{\mathbf{k}}
is Galois over
\mathbf{k}
.

Recall that, giving two Galois extensions
\mathbf{E}_{1}
and
\mathbf{E}_{2}
of a field
\mathbf{k}
, their
composite

\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{E}_{2}
in
\overline{\mathbf{k}}
is the smallest subfield of
\overline{\mathbf{k}}
that contains both
\mathbf{E}_{1}
and
\mathbf{E}_{2}
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\mathbf{E}_{1}
and
\mathbf{E}_{2}
be two Galois extensions of a field
\mathbf{k}
. The homomorphism

\psi\colon\Gal(\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{E}_{2}/\mathbf{k})\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\Gal(\mathbf{E}_{1}/\mathbf{k})\times\Gal(\mathbf{E}_{2}/\mathbf{k}),\ \ g\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
