{}
\lxSVG@stroke}}
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.52 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.25pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.46 L 0 -3.46}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,(g|_{\mathbf{E}_{1}},g|_{\mathbf{E}_{2}})

is injective and induces an isomorphism

\psi\colon\Gal(\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{E}_{2}/\mathbf{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}}\{(u,v)\in\Gal(\mathbf{E}_{1}/\mathbf{k})\times(\mathbf{E}_{2}/\mathbf{k})\colon u|_{\mathbf{E}_{1}\cap\mathbf{E}_{2}}=v|_{\mathbf{E}_{1}\cap\mathbf{E}_{2}}\}.

In particular,
\psi
is an isomorphism if and only if
\mathbf{E}_{1}\cap\mathbf{E}_{2}=\mathbf{k}
.

Remark 2.3
.

In this paper, Lemma
2.2
will be used either in the situation when
\mathbf{E}_{1}\cap\mathbf{E}_{2}=\mathbf{k}
, so that
\Gal(\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
can be identified with the direct product
\Gal(\mathbf{E}_{1}/\mathbf{k})\times\Gal(\mathbf{E}_{2}/\mathbf{k})
, or when
\mathbf{E}_{1}\cap\mathbf{E}_{2}
is a quadratic extension of
\mathbf{k}
. Specifically, assume that
\Gal(\mathbf{E}_{2}/\mathbf{k})=\langle w,t\rangle\simeq\Sym_{3}
, where
w
and
t
are generators of orders 3 and 2, respectively.

\bullet

Suppose that
\Gal(\mathbf{E}_{1}/\mathbf{k})
is

\langle g,h\ |\ g^{3}=h^{2}=\mathrm{id},\ gh=hg\rangle\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}\ \ \text{or}\ \ \langle g,f\ |\ g^{3}=f^{2}=\mathrm{id},\ fgf=g^{2}\rangle\simeq\Sym_{3}.

Then, by Lemma
2.1
, one has
\mathbf{E}_{1}\cap\mathbf{E}_{2}=\mathbf{E}_{1}^{g}=\mathbf{E}_{2}^{w}
. The group
\Gal(\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{E}_{2}/\mathbf{k})
, under the above embedding
\psi
, is thus generated by
(g,\mathrm{id})
,
(\mathrm{id},w)
and
(h,t)
for
\Gal(\mathbf{E}_{1}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
, and by
(g,\mathrm{id})
,
(\mathrm{id},w)
and
(f,t)
for
\Gal(\mathbf{E}_{1}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\Sym_{3}
.

\bullet

Suppose that

\Gal(\mathbf{E}_{1}/\mathbf{k})=\langle g,f,h\ |\ g^{3}=f^{2}=h^{2}=\mathrm{id},\ hf=fh,\ gh=hg,\ fgf=g^{2}\rangle\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}.

Possible quadratic extensions of
\mathbf{k}
in this case are
\mathbf{E}_{1}^{\langle u,g\rangle}=\mathbf{E}_{2}^{w}
, where
u\in\{h,f,s=hf\}
. The group
\Gal(\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{E}_{2}/\mathbf{k})
, is generated by
(g,\mathrm{id})
,
(\mathrm{id},w)
,
(u,\mathrm{id})
and
1
1

1

In each case, the first entry of this generator can be chosen to be any element whose class in
\mathrm{D}_{6}/\langle g,u\rangle
is not trivial.

(f,t)\ \text{if}\ u=h,\ \ \ \ (h,t)\ \text{if}\ u=f,\ \ \ \ (h,t)\ \text{if}\ u=s.

Finally, given a finite Galois extension
\mathbf{E}_{1}/\mathbf{E}_{2}
, an automorphism
g\in\Gal(\mathbf{E}_{1}/\mathbf{E}_{2})
of order
n
, and an element
\lambda\in\mathbf{E}_{1}
we call the element

\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}g^{k}(\lambda)\in\mathbf{E}_{2}

the
g
-norm
of
\lambda
.

Theorem 2.4

(Hilbert’s Theorem 90,
[
GS17
, Example 2.3.4]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}_{1}/\mathbf{E}_{2}
be a Galois extension of fields with cyclic Galois group
\Gal(\mathbf{E}_{1}/\mathbf{E}_{2})
generated by an element
g
. If
\lambda\in\mathbf{E}_{1}
is an element of
g
-norm 1, i.e.
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=1
, then there is
\mu\in\mathbf{E}_{1}
such that
\lambda=\mu/g(\mu)
.

2.2.
Galois cohomology

We recall some basic facts about Galois cohomology and twisted forms of varieties, following
[
Ser97
, I§5, III§1]
. Given a profinite group
G
and a
G
-group
A
(where the action will be denoted by
g(a)
or simply
ga
for
g\in G
,
a\in A
), one defines a
1-cocycle
as a continuous map
\alpha\colon G\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,A
,
g\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.52 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.25pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.46 L 0 -3.46}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\alpha_{g}
, which satisfies the condition

\alpha_{g_{1}g_{2}}=\alpha_{g_{1}}g_{1}(\alpha_{g_{2}}),\ \ g_{1},g_{2}\in G.

The set of these 1-cocycles is denoted
\Cocycles^{1}(G,A)
. Two cocycles
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
are said to be
cohomologous

\alpha\sim\alpha^{\prime}
if there is
\beta\in A
such that
\alpha_{g}^{\prime}=\beta^{-1}\alpha_{g}g(\beta)
. This is an equivalence relation and the corresponding quotient of
\Cocycles^{1}(G,A)
is the first cohomology (pointed) set
\Cohom^{1}(G,A)
.

Now let
X
be an algebraic variety over
\mathbf{k}
and consider a Galois extension
\mathbf{L}\supset\mathbf{k}
with the Galois group
G=\Gal(\mathbf{L}/\mathbf{k})
. Set
X_{\mathbf{L}}=X\times_{\Spec\mathbf{k}}\Spec{\mathbf{L}}
. Then
\Aut(X_{\mathbf{L}})
is equipped with a continuous action of
G
, given by

(2.1)

\alpha\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.52 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.25pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.46 L 0 -3.46}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,g(\alpha)=(\mathrm{id}\otimes g)\circ\alpha\circ(\mathrm{id}\otimes g^{-1}),\ \ g\in\Gal(\mathbf{L}/\mathbf{k}).

We then consider 1-cocycles with values in
\Aut(X_{\mathbf{L}})
and the corresponding cohomology set

\Cohom^{1}(\Gal(\mathbf{L}/\mathbf{k}),\Aut(X_{\mathbf{L}})).

Recall that a
\mathbf{k}
-variety
Y
is called an
\mathbf{L}/\mathbf{k}
-form
of
X
if
Y_{\mathbf{L}}\simeq X_{\mathbf{L}}
as
\mathbf{L}
-varieties. It turns out that, if
X
is quasiprojective, then the set of
\mathbf{L}/\mathbf{k}
forms of
X
is in bijection with the set
\Cohom^{1}(\Gal(\mathbf{L}/\mathbf{k}),\Aut(X_{\mathbf{L}}))
. More precisely, given an
\mathbf{L}/\mathbf{k}
-form
Y
of
X
, the corresponding 1-cocycle
\alpha
can be chosen as follows. We fix an isomorphism
\varphi\colon Y_{\mathbf{L}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}}X_{\mathbf{L}}
and take
\alpha\colon G\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\Aut(X_{\mathbf{L}})
to be the function such that the action of
G
on
Y_{\mathbf{L}}
induces the
twisted action
on
X_{\mathbf{L}}
via

\varphi\circ(\mathrm{id}\otimes g)\circ\varphi^{-1}=\alpha_{g}\circ(\mathrm{id}\otimes g),

or, in a slightly less formal form which we will use in the future:

(2.2)

\varphi\circ g\circ\varphi^{-1}=\alpha_{g}\circ g.

Note that the cohomology class of
\alpha
does not depend on
\varphi
.

2.3.
Severi-Brauer varieties

Recall that a
Severi-Brauer variety
over
\mathbf{k}
is a projective algebraic variety
X
such that
X_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}=X\times_{\Spec\mathbf{k}}\Spec\overline{\mathbf{k}}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{n}_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}
. Severi-Brauer varieties of dimension
n-1
over
\mathbf{k}
are in one-to-one correspondence with central simple
\mathbf{k}
-algebras of degree
n
as they are both parametrized by the set
\Cohom^{1}(\Gal(\mathbf{k}^{\rm sep}/\mathbf{k}),\PGL_{n}(\mathbf{k}^{\rm sep}))
. In particular, if a Severi-Brauer variety
X
corresponds to a central simple algebra
A
, then one can define the
opposite
variety
X^{\rm op}
as the variety which corresponds to the inverse of
A
in the Brauer group
\Br(\mathbf{k})
.

A Severi–Brauer variety
X
always splits (becomes
trivial
) over a finite Galois extension
\mathbf{L}
of the base field
\mathbf{k}
, i.e. one has
X_{\mathbf{L}}\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{L}}^{n}
, see e.g.
[
GS17
, Corollary 5.1.5]
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
X
be a non-trivial Severi-Brauer surface over a field
\mathbf{k}
. Then one has the following.

(1)

The surface
X
does not contain points of degree not divisible by
3
.

(2)

The surface
X
always contains a closed point of degree
3
.

(3)

Any point of degree 3 or 6 on
S
is in general position.

Proof.

See
[
Kol16
, Theorem 53]
for the first two claims, and
[
Shr20
, Lemma 2.6]
for the third one.
∎

The following statement is one of the crucial birational properties of non-trivial Severi-Brauer surfaces.

Proposition 2.6

(
[
Wei89
]
, also follows from
[
Isk96
, Theorem 2.6]
)
.

Let
S
be a non-trivial Severi-Brauer surface over
\mathbf{k}
and
S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S^{\prime}
be a birational map where
S^{\prime}
is a del Pezzo surface with
\rk\Pic(S^{\prime})=1
. Then either
S^{\prime}\simeq S
, or
S^{\prime}\simeq S^{\rm op}
.

In what follows, we will need several results on explicit parametrization of Severi-Brauer curves and surfaces. Since we were unable to find these results in the literature, we provide their complete proofs below.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\mathbf{L}/\mathbf{k}
be an extension of degree
2
, with
\Gal(\mathbf{L}/\mathbf{k})=\langle h\rangle
. Let
C
be a Severi-Brauer curve defined over
\mathbf{k}
that splits over
\mathbf{L}
. There is an isomorphism
\varphi\colon C_{\mathbf{L}}\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{L}}
such that the twisted action (
2.2
) is given by

\alpha_{h}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\delta\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\in\PGL_{2}(\mathbf{L})

for
\delta\in\mathbf{k}^{*}
and every
\delta\in\mathbf{k}^{*}
gives a Severi-Brauer curve
C_{\delta}
. Moreover,
C_{\delta}\simeq C_{\delta^{\prime}}
if and only if
\delta^{\prime}/\delta\in\Norm_{h}(\mathbf{L}^{*})
.

Proof.

Let
\{p,h(p)\}
be a point of degree 2 on
C
. Given an isomorphism
\varphi\colon C_{\mathbf{L}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}}\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{L}}
, we can suppose (possibly composing with an automorphism of
\mathbb{P}_{\textbf{L}}^{1}
), that
\varphi(p)=[1:0]
,
\varphi(h(p))=[0:1]
. This implies that the element
\alpha_{h}\in\PGL_{2}(\mathbf{L})
satisfying
\varphi\circ h\circ\varphi^{-1}=\alpha_{h}\circ h
is of the form

\alpha_{h}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\delta\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}

for some
\delta\in\mathbf{L}^{*}
. The cocycle condition is
\alpha_{h}\cdot h(\alpha_{h})=\mathrm{id}
, which corresponds to
\delta\in\mathbf{k}^{*}
.

Let
\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbf{k}^{*}
be such that
\delta^{\prime}=\Norm_{h}(\lambda)\delta
for some
\lambda\in L^{*}
, and put

\alpha_{h}^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\delta^{\prime}\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\ \ \beta=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

Then
\beta\alpha_{h}h(\beta^{-1})=h(\lambda)^{-1}\alpha_{h}^{\prime}
, i.e. the two 1-cocycles given by
\alpha_{h}
and
\alpha_{h}^{\prime}
are equivalent, hence
C_{\delta}\simeq C_{\delta^{\prime}}
. Conversely, if the two 1-cycles are equivalent then there is matrix
\beta\in\GL_{2}(\mathbf{L})
and an element
\lambda\in\mathbf{L}^{*}
such that
\lambda\alpha_{h}=\beta\alpha_{h}^{\prime}h(\beta^{-1})
. Therefore,

\lambda h(\lambda)\delta\mathrm{I}_{2}=(\lambda\alpha_{h})\cdot h(\lambda\alpha_{h})=\beta\alpha_{h}^{\prime}h(\alpha_{h}^{\prime})\beta^{-1}=\delta^{\prime}\mathrm{I}_{2},

hence the claim.
∎

Lemma 2.8

(
[
BSY22
, Lemmas 2.3.3 and 3.2.9]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k}
be a Galois extension of degree
3
, with
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})=\langle g\rangle
.
There is a one-to-one correspondence between Severi-Brauer surfaces over
\mathbf{k}
that split over
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{k}^{*}/\Norm_{g}(\mathbf{E}^{*})
. For
\xi\in\mathbf{k}^{*}
and
X_{\xi}
the corresponding Severi-Brauer surface, there is an isomorphism
\varphi_{\xi}:(X_{\xi})_{\mathbf{E}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}}\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\mathbf{E}}
such that the twisted action (
2.2
) is given by

\alpha_{g}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\xi\\
1&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix}\in\PGL_{3}(\mathbf{E}).

Moreover, if
X
corresponds to
\xi\in\mathbf{k}^{*}
, then
X^{\rm op}
corresponds to
\xi^{-1}
.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k}
be a Galois extension, with

\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})=\langle s,g\ |\ g^{3}=s^{2}=(sg)^{2}=\mathrm{id}\rangle\simeq\Sym_{3}.

(1)

For each
\tau\in\mathbf{E}^{*}
such that
\xi=\tau s(\tau)sg(\tau)\in\mathbf{E}^{g}
, there is a unique Severi-Brauer surface
X_{\xi}
defined over
\mathbf{k}
and an isomorphism
\varphi\colon(X_{\xi})_{\mathbf{E}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{E}}^{2}
such that the twisted action (
2.2
) is given by

\alpha_{g}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\xi\\
1&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix},\ \ \ \alpha_{s}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&\tau\\
0&s(\tau^{-1})&0\end{pmatrix}.

(2)

Every Severi-Brauer surface defined over
\mathbf{k}
that admits a
3
-point that splits over
\mathbf{E}
is isomorphic to a surface
X_{\xi}
as in
(1)
.

(3)

Two surfaces
X_{\xi}
and
X_{\xi^{\prime}}
as in
(1)
are isomorphic over
\mathbf{k}
if and only if

\frac{\xi^{\prime}}{\xi}\in\Norm_{g}(\mathbf{E}^{*}).

In particular,
X_{\xi}
is trivial if and only
\xi\in\Norm_{g}(\mathbf{E}^{*})
.

Proof.

(1)
To check that such a surface exists, we need to verify that there is a cocycle
r\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.52 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.25pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.46 L 0 -3.46}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\alpha_{r}
, or equivalently that
r\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.52 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.25pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.46 L 0 -3.46}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\alpha_{r}\circ r
is a group homomorphism from
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
to
\PGL_{3}(\mathbf{E})\rtimes\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
. As
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
is generated by
g
and
s
, the elements
\alpha_{g}
and
\alpha_{s}
determine the group homomorphism (or the cocycle). The relation
g^{3}=\mathrm{id}
corresponds to
\alpha_{g}g(\alpha_{g})g^{2}(\alpha_{g})=\alpha_{g^{3}}=\mathrm{id}\in\PGL_{3}(\mathbf{E})
and is equivalent to
g(\xi)=\xi
. The relation
\alpha_{s}s(\alpha_{s})=\mathrm{id}
is implied by the form of
\alpha_{s}
and the relation
gs=sg^{2}
corresponds to

\alpha_{gs}=\alpha_{g}g(\alpha_{s})=\alpha_{sg^{2}}=\alpha_{s}s(\alpha_{g^{2}})=\alpha_{s}s(\alpha_{g})\cdot sg(\alpha_{g})

and thus, remembering that
g(\xi)=\xi
, to the equality

\begin{pmatrix}0&\xi sg^{2}(\tau^{-1})&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&g(\tau)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&s(\xi)&0\\
\tau&0&0\\
0&0&s(\xi)s(\tau^{-1})\end{pmatrix}\in\PGL_{3}(\mathbf{E}).

This is equivalent to

\tau\xi=s(\xi)sg^{2}(\tau),\quad s(\xi)=s(\tau)\tau g(\tau).

Applying
s
to the second equality, we obtain
\xi=\tau s(\tau)sg(\tau)
. Replacing it in the first equality, we obtain
\tau^{2}s(\tau)sg(\tau)=s(\tau)\tau g(\tau)sg^{2}(\tau),
which is equivalent to
\tau sg(\tau)=g(\tau)sg^{2}(\tau),
and also to the equality
g(\xi)=\xi
.

(2)

Let
X
be a Severi-Brauer surface defined over
\mathbf{k}
and let
\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\}\subset X(\mathbf{E})
be a
3
-point that splits over
\mathbf{E}
. By changing the order, we may suppose that
g(p_{1})=p_{2}
and
s(p_{1})=p_{1}
, which implies that
g
and
s
correspond to
(123)
and
(23)
. We can choose
\varphi
so that
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}
are sent onto
[1:0:0],[0:1:0],[0:0:1]
, respectively. Thus
\alpha_{g}
is of the form
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0&\xi\\
\beta&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, for some
\xi,\beta\in\mathbf{E}^{*}
. Replacing
\varphi
with its composition by a diagonal automorphism, we may assume that
\beta=1
. We then write
\alpha_{s}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&\tau\\
0&\kappa&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
with
\tau,\kappa\in\mathbf{E}^{*}
. As
\alpha_{s}s(\alpha_{s})=\alpha_{s^{2}}=\mathrm{id}\in\PGL_{3}(\mathbf{E})
we find
\kappa=s(\tau^{-1})
and obtain the form as in
(1)
, where
\xi=\tau s(\tau)sg(\tau)
and
g(\xi)=\xi
are given by the fact that we have a cocycle, as explained above.

(3)
We take two Severi-Brauer surfaces
X_{\xi}
and
X_{\xi^{\prime}}
as in
(1)
, induced by
\xi,\tau\in\mathbf{E}
and
\xi^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}\in\mathbf{E}
respectively, with
\xi=\tau s(\tau)sg(\tau)
,
\xi^{\prime}=\tau^{\prime}s(\tau^{\prime})sg(\tau^{\prime})\in\mathbf{E}^{g}
.

\bullet

Step 1.
We claim that if there is
\lambda\in\mathbf{E}^{*}
such that

\tau^{\prime}=\tau s(\lambda)sg(\lambda){\lambda^{-1}},

then
X_{\xi}
and
X_{\xi^{\prime}}
are isomorphic
2
2

2

In fact, this condition is both necessary and sufficient.
. Indeed, we have

\xi^{\prime}=\tau^{\prime}s(\tau^{\prime})sg(\tau^{\prime})=\tau s(\tau)sg(\tau)\lambda g(\lambda)g^{2}(\lambda)=\xi\Norm_{g}(\lambda).

Define
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&\lambda&0\\
0&0&\lambda g(\lambda)\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\PGL_{3}(\mathbf{E})
and calculate
3
3

3

The equality here should be understood as equality in
\PGL_{3}(\mathbf{E})
.

M^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\xi\\
1&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix}g(M)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\xi^{\prime}\\
1&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix},\ \ M^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&\tau\\
0&s(\tau^{-1})&0\end{pmatrix}s(M)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&\tau^{\prime}\\
0&s(\tau^{\prime-1})&0\end{pmatrix},

which implies that
X_{\xi}
and
X_{\xi^{\prime}}
are isomorphic.

\bullet

Step 2.
If
\xi=\xi^{\prime}
, we write
\theta=\tau^{\prime}/\tau\in\mathbf{E}^{*}
and obtain
\theta s(\theta)sg(\theta)=1
, since
\tau s(\tau)sg(\tau)=\xi=\xi^{\prime}=\tau^{\prime}s(\tau^{\prime})sg(\tau^{\prime})
. By choosing
\lambda=sg(\theta)=\theta^{-1}s(\theta^{-1})\in\mathbf{E}^{s}
, we obtain
\lambda=g(\theta)
and then

s(\lambda)sg(\lambda){\lambda^{-1}}=sg(\lambda)=\theta
. We then obtain
\tau^{\prime}=\tau s(\lambda)sg(\lambda){\lambda^{-1}}
as in Step 1 and thus
X_{\xi}
and
X_{\xi^{\prime}}
are isomorphic. This explains why the surface only depends on
\xi
and thus motivates the notation
X_{\xi}
.

\bullet

Step 3.
We now show the sufficiency in
(3)
. If there is
\mu\in\mathbf{E}^{*}
such that
\xi^{\prime}=\Norm_{g}(\mu)\xi
, we write

\widetilde{\tau}=\tau^{\prime}\frac{\mu}{s(\mu)sg(\mu)},

and deduce

\widetilde{\tau}s(\widetilde{\tau})sg(\widetilde{\tau})=\tau^{\prime}s(\tau^{\prime})sg(\tau^{\prime})\frac{\mu}{s(\mu)sg(\mu)}\frac{s(\mu)}{\mu g(\mu)}\frac{sg(\mu)}{g^{2}(\mu)\mu}=\frac{\xi^{\prime}}{\Norm_{g}(\mu)}=\xi\in\mathbf{E}^{g}.

So, we obtain a Severi-Brauer
X_{\xi}
given by
(\xi,\widetilde{\tau})
. By Step 2, this surface is isomorphic to
X_{\xi}
, given by
(\xi,\tau)
, and by Step 1 the surface is isomorphic to
X_{\xi^{\prime}}
.

\bullet

Step 4.
Finally, we show the necessity in
(3)
. Suppose that
X_{\xi}
and
X_{\xi^{\prime}}
are isomorphic. We proceed as in the proof of
[
BSY22
, Lemma 2.3.3 (4)]
. Write
R_{\xi}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0&\xi\\
1&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\GL_{3}(\mathbf{E}^{g})
and
R_{\xi^{\prime}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0&\xi^{\prime}\\
1&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\GL_{3}(\mathbf{E}^{g})
and obtain

A\in\GL_{3}(\mathbf{E})
and
a\in\mathbf{E}^{*}
such that
aR_{\xi}=A^{-1}R_{\xi^{\prime}}g(A)
. Hence, we find

A=a^{-1}R_{\xi}g(A)R_{\xi^{\prime}}^{-1}.

Applying
g
and
g^{2}
gives
g(A)={g(a)^{-1}}R_{\xi}g^{2}(A)R_{\xi^{\prime}}^{-1}
and
g^{2}(A)={g^{2}(a)^{-1}}R_{\xi}AR_{\xi^{\prime}}^{-1}
. We then replace successively
A
,
g(A)
and
g^{2}(A)
and obtain

ag(a)g^{2}(a)A=g(a)g^{2}(a)R_{\xi}g(A)R_{\xi^{\prime}}^{-1}=g^{2}(a)R_{\xi}^{2}g^{2}(A)R_{\xi^{\prime}}^{-2}=R_{\xi}^{3}AR_{\xi^{\prime}}^{-3}=\xi\xi^{\prime-1}A.

This yields
\xi^{\prime}/\xi=ag(a)g^{2}(a)\in\Norm_{g}(\mathbf{E}^{*})
, as required.

We have proven that
X_{\xi}
and
X_{\xi^{\prime}}
are isomorphic if and only if
\xi^{\prime}/\xi\in\Norm_{g}(\mathbf{E}^{*})
. To finish the proof, it remains to check that
X_{1}
is trivial. For this take
\tau=1
to get
X_{\xi}\simeq X_{1}
, and observe that
[1:1:1]
is fixed by
\varphi\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})\varphi^{-1}
, and thus
\varphi^{-1}([1:1:1])
is an
\mathbf{E}
-point of
S
fixed by the Galois group
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
, so it is a
\mathbf{k}
-rational point.

∎

2.4.
Involution surfaces

In this section, we recall the birational properties of del Pezzo surfaces
S
, such that
S_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}^{1}
, also known as
involution surfaces
. We follow recent works of A. Trepain
[
Tre23
]
and J.-L. Colliot-Thélène
[
CT21
]
. First of all, let us state the biregular classification of such surfaces. Note that we have
\rk\Pic(S)\in\{1,2\}
, as
\Pic(S)\subseteq\Pic(S_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}})
.

Proposition 2.10

(
[
SV18
, Lemma 7.3]
)
.

Let
S
be a del Pezzo surface of degree 8 such that
S_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}^{1}
. Then one of the following cases holds:

(1)

If
\rk\Pic(S)=1
, then
S\simeq\Weil_{\mathbf{L}/\mathbf{k}}C
, the Weil restriction of scalars for a conic
C
over a quadratic extension
\mathbf{L}/\mathbf{k}
. The extension
\mathbf{L}/\mathbf{k}
and the conic
C
are uniquely determined by
S
up to conjugation by
\Gal(\mathbf{L}/\mathbf{k})
.

(2)

If
\rk\Pic(S)=2
, then
S\simeq C_{1}\times C_{2}
, where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are two conics over
\mathbf{k}
, uniquely determined by
S
.

Moreover,
S
is isomorphic to a quadric in
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{k}}^{3}
if and only if
S\simeq C\times C
or
S\simeq\Weil_{\mathbf{L}/\mathbf{k}}C_{\mathbf{L}}
for some conic
C
over
\mathbf{k}
.

We then focus on del Pezzo surfaces
S
of degree 8 with
\rk\Pic(S)=2
. By Proposition
2.10
, one has
S\simeq C_{1}\times C_{1}
, where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are two conics over
\mathbf{k}
, uniquely determined by
S
. The description of possible birational models of
S
in the non-rational case (i.e. when
S(\mathbf{k})=\varnothing
) is due to J. Kollàr
[
Kol05
]
and A. Trepalin
[
Tre23
]
.

Definition 2.11

(
[
Kol05
, 7]
)
.

Let
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
be two smooth conics over
\mathbf{k}
, and
b(C_{1})
and
b(C_{2})
be their classes in the Brauer group
\Br(\mathbf{k})
. If there exists a conic
C_{3}
such that
b(C_{3})=b(C_{1})+b(C_{2})
, then
C_{3}
is called the
Brauer product

C_{1}*C_{2}
of
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
.

Proposition 2.12

(
[
Tre23
, Lemma 3.2]
and
[
Kol05
, Lemma 8]
)
.

The Brauer product
C_{1}*C_{2}
is defined if and only if there exists a quadratic extension
\mathbf{L}/\mathbf{k}
, such that both
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
have closed points of degree 2 whose splitting field is
\mathbf{L}
; equivalently, the Severi-Brauer curves
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are trivial over
\mathbf{L}
.

Theorem 2.13

(
[
Tre23
, Theorem 1.5]
,
[
Kol05
, Example 6]
)
.

Let
S
be a del Pezzo surface of degree 8 over a perfect field
\mathbf{k}
such that
S_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}^{1}
. Suppose that
S(\mathbf{k})=\varnothing
and
\rk\Pic(S)=2
. Then
S
is a product of two smooth conics and one of the following holds:

(1)

S\simeq C\times C
or
S\simeq C\times\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{k}}^{1}
, where
C(\mathbf{k})=\varnothing
. Let
S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S^{\prime}
be a birational map to another Mori fibre space
S^{\prime}
. Then
S^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
C\times C
, or to
C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{k}}
, or to a
\mathbf{k}
-form of a Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{2k}
over
C
.

(2)

S\simeq C_{1}\times C_{2}
, where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are two conics with no
\mathbf{k}
-points, not isomorphic to each other. Let
S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S^{\prime}
be a birational map to another Mori fibre space
S^{\prime}
. If the Brauer product
C_{3}=C_{1}*C_{2}
is not defined, then
S^{\prime}\simeq C_{1}\times C_{2}
. Otherwise
S^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
C_{1}\times C_{2}
, or to
C_{1}\times C_{3}
, or to
C_{2}\times C_{3}
.

2.5.
The Sarkisov program

The main tool for studying groups of birational self-maps
\Bir_{\mathbf{k}}(S)
is the so-called
Sarkisov program
, which allows to decompose every birational map
S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S^{\prime}
between Mori fiber spaces (see below) into a composition of simpler maps, known as
Sarkisov links
. We recall the basics of this theory very briefly and refer to
[
LZ20
]
and
[
BLZ21
]
for more details.

Let
S
be a surface, and
r\geqslant 1
an integer.
We say that
S
is a
rank
r
fibration
if there exists a surjective morphism
\pi\colon S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,B
with connected fibers, where
B
is a point or a smooth curve, with relative Picard number equal to
r
, and such that the anticanonical divisor
-K_{S}
is
\pi
-ample. We will write
S/B
if we want to emphasize the base of the fibration. An isomorphism between two fibrations
S/B
and
S^{\prime}/B^{\prime}
of the same rank
r
is an isomorphism
S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}}S^{\prime}
such that there exists an isomorphism on the bases (necessarily uniquely defined) that makes the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}}

We say that a rank
r
fibration
S/B

factorises through
a rank
r^{\prime}
fibration
S^{\prime}/B^{\prime}
, or that
S^{\prime}/B^{\prime}
is dominated by
S/B
, if the fibrations
S/B
and
S^{\prime}/B^{\prime}
fit in a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}

where
S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S^{\prime}
is a birational contraction, and
B^{\prime}\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,B
is a morphism with connected fibers. Note that
r\geqslant r^{\prime}
.

Rank 1 fibrations are simply 2-dimensional Mori fiber spaces in the sense of Mori theory (see e.g.
[
BLZ21
, Lemma 3.3]
). So, geometrically rational Mori fiber spaces in dimension 2 are exactly del Pezzo surfaces
S
with
\Pic(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
and conic bundles
\pi\colon S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,B
with
\Pic(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

The usual notion of Sarkisov links between 2-dimensional Mori fiber spaces can be rephrased in terms of rank
2
fibrations. Namely, a 2-ray game allows to show
[
BLZ21
, Lemma 3.7]
that a rank
2
fibration
T/B
factorises through exactly two rank
1
fibrations
S/B
and
S^{\prime}/B^{\prime}
(up to isomorphisms). The induced birational map
\chi:S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S^{\prime}
is called a
Sarkisov link
. In this paper, we will mostly consider
links of type
\mathrm{II}
between del Pezzo surfaces. These are diagrams

(2.3)

\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Spec\mathbf{k}}

where
S
and
S^{\prime}
are both minimal del Pezzo surfaces, and
B=\Spec\mathbf{k}
.

The notion of rank
3
fibration recovers the notion of an
elementary relation
between Sarkisov links.

Proposition 2.14

(
[
BLZ21
, Proposition 4.3]
)
.

Let
Z/B
be a rank
3
fibration.
Then there are only finitely many Sarkisov links
\chi_{i}
dominated by
Z/B
, and they fit in a relation

(2.4)

\chi_{t}\circ\dots\circ\chi_{1}=\mathrm{id}.

In this situation, we say that (
2.4
) is an
elementary relation
between Sarkisov links, coming from the rank
3
fibration
Z/B
.
Observe that the elementary relation is uniquely defined by
Z/B
, up to taking the inverse, cyclic permutations and insertion of isomorphisms.

Let
S/B
a Mori fiber space. We denote by
\BirMori(S)
the
groupoid
of birational maps between Mori fiber spaces birational to
S
. Note that
\Bir(S)
is a subgroupoid of
\BirMori(S)
. We now state the crucial technical result that we are going to use in this paper. Its first part is known as the
Sarkisov program
, which in most general form was proven by C. Hacon and J. McKernan
[
HM13
]
, and by Iskovskikh in dimension 2
[
Isk96
]
. The second part is inspired by A.-S. Kaloghiros
[
Kal13
, Theorem 1.3]
and was proven in
[
LZ20
,
BLZ21
]
.

Theorem 2.15

(
Sarkisov program).

Let
S/B
be a Mori fiber space (of dimension 2).

(1)

The groupoid
\BirMori(S)
is generated by Sarkisov links and automorphisms.

(2)

Any relation between Sarkisov links in
\BirMori(S)
is generated by trivial and elementary relations.

Trivial relations will be defined in Section
7
.

3.
Biregular classification

Building on the construction
[
CTKM08
]
of J.-L. Colliot-Thélène, N. Karpenko and A. Merkurjev, in
[
Blu10
, Theorem 3.4]
M. Blunk gives an algebraic way to classify sextic del Pezzo surfaces
S
over a field
\mathbf{k}
. To every such surface, one associates a pair of separable
\mathbf{k}
-algebras
B
and
Q
, defined as endomorphism rings of
certain locally free sheaves on
S
. The algebras
B
and
Q
are Azumaya over their centers, which are étale quadratic and cubic extensions of
\mathbf{k}
, respectively, and can be recovered from the action of
\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{k}}/\mathbf{k})
on the
(-1)
-curves of
S
. We also refer to
[
AB18
, Table 4]
for the list of possible Blunk’s data and corresponding arithmetic invariants of
S
.

Likewise, starting from the action of the Galois group on the
(-1)
-curves and some extensions of
\mathbf{k}
associated with this action, we provide below a particularly transparent description of sextic del Pezzo surfaces
S
with
\Pic(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
from a birational perspective. Our primary motivation for this approach is to understand the birational models of
S
, their Sarkisov links and birational self-maps.

3.1.
The blow-up model over
\overline{\mathbf{k}}

Let
\mathbf{k}
be a perfect field and
S
be a del Pezzo surface of degree
K_{S}^{2}=6
over
\mathbf{k}
. Recall that
S_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}
is the blow-up

and

on the hexagon (
3.2
), i.e. as
\Phi(\theta)
.

The element
(1,\mathrm{id})\in\mathbb{Z}/2\times\Sym_{3}
sends the faces of
\Sigma
to their opposites, i.e. acts as
\Phi(\iota)
. The element
(1,(23))
acts as
\Phi(\sigma)
. We take, if they exist,

h=\epsilon^{-1}((1,\mathrm{id})),\ \ g=\epsilon^{-1}((0,(123))),\ \ f=\epsilon^{-1}((1,(23))).

Definition 3.1
.

Given a subgroup
G\subset\mathrm{D}_{6}
, we call
a sextic
G
-del Pezzo surface
a del Pezzo surface
S
of degree 6 over
\mathbf{k}
such that
\Pic(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
and the splitting field
\mathbf{F}
of its hexagon has
G
as its Galois group over
\mathbf{k}
, if such surface exists.

Remark 3.2
.

Recall that by Iskovskikh’s rationality criterion
[
Isk96
, p. 642]
, a geometrically rational
\mathbf{k}
-minimal surface
S
is
\mathbf{k}
-rational if and only if
K_{S}^{2}\geqslant 5
and
S(\mathbf{k})\neq\varnothing
. In particular, a sextic
G
-del Pezzo surface
S
is
\mathbf{k}
-rational if and only if
S(\mathbf{k})\neq\varnothing
.

Notation 3.3
.

Since we assume
\Pic(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
, there are only 3 possibilities for the group
G
, namely
G=\langle r\rangle\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
,
G=\langle r^{2},s\rangle\simeq\Sym_{3}
and
G=\langle r,s\rangle\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
. In what follows, it will be convenient to choose the following presentations
4
4

4

It is not immediately clear why we take 2 (respectively, 3) generators for
\mathbb{Z}/6
(respectively, for
\mathrm{D}_{6}
). However, this will simplify many formulas in our further calculations.
of these groups, using the Galois automorphisms from above (by abuse of notation, we denote
\epsilon(u)
by
u
):

\bullet

G=\langle g,h\ |\ g^{3}=h^{2}=\mathrm{id},\ gh=hg\rangle
when
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6
.

\bullet

G=\langle g,f\ |\ g^{3}=f^{2}=\mathrm{id},\ fgf=g^{2}\rangle
when
G\simeq\Sym_{3}
.

\bullet

G=\langle g,f,h\ |\ g^{3}=f^{2}=h^{2}=\mathrm{id},\ hf=fh,\ gh=hg,\ fgf=g^{2}\rangle
when
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
.

In the last case, we set
s=fh
.

The corresponding actions of
G
are shown on Figure
1
.

.

3.2.
Severi-Brauer data

The action of the Galois group
G=\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})
on
\Sigma
induces the following two actions:

\bullet

The group
G
acts on the set of triples
\{\{E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}\},\{F_{1},F_{2},F_{3}\}\}
, with a transitive action, as we assume
\Pic(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. The stabilizer in
G
of
\{E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}\}
gives rise to a quadratic extension
\mathbf{K}/\mathbf{k}
, over which this triple is defined. Note that
\{F_{1},F_{2},F_{3}\}
is then also defined over
\mathbf{K}
. There are two
\mathbf{K}
-blow-downs

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi}
\textstyle{X^{\rm op}}

where
X
is a Severi-Brauer surface, the birational morphism
\eta
is the contraction of the triple
\{E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}\}
onto a zero-dimensional
\mathbf{K}
-subscheme of
X
, and
\eta^{\prime}
is the contraction of the triple
\{F_{1},F_{2},F_{3}\}
onto a zero-dimensional
\mathbf{K}
-subscheme of
X^{\rm op}
, see
[
Cor05
, Proposition 2.1, Theorem 2.3]
and notice that characteristic zero assumption of loc. cit. is redundant, as del Pezzo surfaces are
separably split. Note that
X
uniquely determines
X^{\rm op}
.

\bullet

Furthermore,
G
acts on the set of pairs
\{\{E_{1},F_{1}\},\{E_{2},F_{2}\},\{E_{3},F_{3}\}\}
, with a transitive action, as
\Pic(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. For each
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, there exists thus a cubic extension
\mathbf{L}_{i}/\mathbf{k}
over which some pair
\{E_{i},F_{i}\}
is defined. Over this extension, we get a contraction
S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,Y_{i}
, where
Y_{i}
is an involution surface over the field
\mathbf{L}_{i}
. The extension
\mathbf{L}_{i}/\mathbf{k}
is not necessarily Galois.

The described birational contractions serve as a starting point in the constructions of
[
Blu10
,
CTKM08
]
. To work with Galois cohomology, we will need to define similar data in more concrete terms, using Notation
3.3
.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
S
be a
G
-del Pezzo surface of degree 6, where
G
is as in Notation
3.3
. A
Severi-Brauer data
for
S
consists of:

\bullet

G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
: A Severi-Brauer surface
X
over the field
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{F}^{g}
, such that
S_{\mathbf{K}}
is birational to
X
, and an involution surface
Y
over the field
\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{F}^{h}
such that
S_{\mathbf{L}}
is birational to
Y
.

\bullet

G\simeq\Sym_{3}
: A Severi-Brauer surface
X
over the field
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{F}^{g}
such that
S_{\mathbf{K}}
is birational to
X
.

\bullet

G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
: A Severi-Brauer surface
X
over the field
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,s\rangle}
, such that
S_{\mathbf{K}}
is birational to
X
, and an involution surface
Y
over the field
\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{F}^{h}
such that
S_{\mathbf{L}}
is birational to
Y
.

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be two sextic
G
-del Pezzo surfaces with the same splitting field
\mathbf{F}
. We say that their Severi-Brauer data are
equivalent
, if
X
is
\mathbf{K}
-birational to
X^{\prime}
and
Y
is
\mathbf{L}
-birational to
Y^{\prime}
.

Remark 3.5
.

Let
S
be a sextic
G
-del Pezzo surface over
\mathbf{k}
.

(1)

A Severi-Brauer data for
S
exists, as follows from the discussion in the beginning of the paragraph, but is not uniquely defined: for example, there are two possible choices for the Severi-Brauer surface,
X
and
X^{\rm op}
, and also several choices for an involution surface
Y
, as will be discussed below.

(2)

The extensions
\mathbf{K}/\mathbf{k}
in Definition
3.4
are all of degree 2. The extension
\mathbf{L}/\mathbf{k}
is of degree 3 for
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
and of degree 6 for
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
.
Note that
\rk_{\mathbf{L}}\Pic(Y)=2
for
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
and
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
.

(3)

Recall that
\mathrm{D}_{6}
has four subgroups isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2
. One of them is the normal subgroup
\langle h\rangle
, and the other three
\langle f_{i}\rangle
,
i\in\{1,2,3\}
are conjugate, where
f_{1}=f
,
f_{2}=gf
,
f_{3}=g^{2}f=fg
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle f_{1}

\displaystyle=f\quad

\displaystyle\text{swaps the sections}\ x_{2}=x_{3}=0\ \text{and}\ y_{2}=y_{3}=0,

\displaystyle f_{2}

\displaystyle=gf\quad

\displaystyle\text{swaps the sections}\ x_{1}=x_{2}=0\ \text{and}\ y_{1}=y_{2}=0,

\displaystyle f_{3}

\displaystyle=fg\quad

\displaystyle\text{swaps the sections}\ x_{1}=x_{3}=0\ \text{and}\ y_{1}=y_{3}=0.

We denote
\mathrm{V}_{i}=\langle h,f_{i}\rangle
the three Klein 4-groups. Hence, for
\mathrm{D}_{6}
-del Pezzo surfaces there are three cubic extensions
\mathbf{L}_{i}=\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{V}_{i}}\supset\mathbf{k}
over which we have contractions to involution surfaces
Y_{1}
,
Y_{2}
and
Y_{3}
. In this case,
\rk_{\mathbf{L}_{i}}\Pic(Y_{i})=1
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
.

Remark 3.6
.

In the case of
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
and
G\simeq\Sym_{3}
there is only one embedding
\epsilon
of
G=\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})
into
\mathrm{D}_{6}
, up to conjugacy. In the case of
G=\mathrm{D}_{6}
there are up to conjugation two embeddings which corresponds to switching the roles of the elements called
f,s
. Since
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,s\rangle}
, the Severi-Brauer data uniquely gives the embedding.

3.3.
Sextic
\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
-del Pezzo surfaces

In this Section, we suppose that
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
and find explicit parametrization of such del Pezzo surfaces via Galois cohomology. Throughout this paper, we use the split exact sequence
3.4
and denote the automorphisms of
S_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}
by pairs
((\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\delta)\in T_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}\rtimes\mathrm{D}_{6}
.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
S
be a sextic
\mathbb{Z}/6
-del Pezzo surface. Then the twisted action (
2.2
) can be chosen so that

\alpha_{g}=((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(g)),\ \ \alpha_{h}=((\rho,\rho g(\rho)),\epsilon(h))

for

(3.9)

\xi\in(\mathbf{F}^{g})^{*},\ \ \rho\in(\mathbf{F}^{h})^{*},\ \ \Norm_{h}(\xi)\Norm_{g}(\rho)=1.

Moreover every such pair
\xi,\rho
yields a del Pezzo surface with said properties.

Proof.

The Galois group
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/3
is generated by
(h,g)
. Letting

\alpha_{g}=((\xi_{1},\xi_{2}),\epsilon(g)),\ \ \alpha_{h}=((\rho_{1},\rho_{2}),\epsilon(h))

we easily observe that this association defines a
1
-cocycle. We want to use the relations to get more information on the coefficients
\xi_{i},\rho_{i}
.

First, we use that
g^{3}=\mathrm{id}
. One has

g(\alpha_{g})=((g(\xi_{1}),g(\xi_{2})),\epsilon(g)),

hence, using the semidirect product structure (
3.8
), one gets

\alpha_{g^{2}}=\alpha_{g}g(\alpha_{g})=\left(\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{g(\xi_{2})},\frac{\xi_{2}g(\xi_{1})}{g(\xi_{2})}\right),\epsilon(g)^{2}\right).

Therefore,

((1,1),\mathrm{id})=\alpha_{g^{3}}=\alpha_{g}g(\alpha_{g^{2}})=\left(\left(\frac{\xi_{1}g^{2}(\xi_{2})}{g^{2}(\xi_{1})g(\xi_{2})},\frac{\xi_{2}g(\xi_{1})}{g(\xi_{2})g^{2}(\xi_{1})}\right),\mathrm{id}\right)

which is equivalent to
\xi_{2}g(\xi_{1})\in(\mathbf{F}^{g})^{*}
. Put
\xi=\xi_{2}g(\xi_{1})
and replace
\varphi
with
((\xi_{1}^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\mathrm{id})\circ\varphi
, which replaces
\alpha_{g}
with
((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(g))
.

Next, we use
h^{2}=\mathrm{id}
. One has
h(\alpha_{h})=((h(\rho_{1}),h(\rho_{2})),\epsilon(h))
, which gives

((1,1),\mathrm{id})=\alpha_{h^{2}}=\alpha_{h}h(\alpha_{h})=\left(\left(\frac{\rho_{1}}{h(\rho_{1})},\frac{\rho_{2}}{h(\rho_{2})}\right),\mathrm{id}\right)

and thus
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in({\mathbf{F}}^{h})^{*}
.

Finally, we use the relation
gh=hg
, which gives
\alpha_{g}g(\alpha_{h})=\alpha_{h}h(a_{g})
. Hence, by using the actions (
3.8
), we get

\left(\left(\rho_{1}h(\xi),\rho_{2}h(\xi)\right),\epsilon(gh)\right)=\left(\left(\frac{1}{\xi g(\rho_{2})},\frac{g(\rho_{1})}{\xi g(\rho_{2})}\right),\epsilon(hg)\right)

which is equivalent to
\rho_{2}=\rho_{1}g(\rho_{1})
,
\Norm_{h}(\xi)\Norm_{g}(\rho_{1})=1
. Letting
\rho=\rho_{1}
, we get a pair
(\xi,\rho)\in(\mathbf{F}^{g})^{*}\times(\mathbf{F}^{h})^{*}
as in the statement.
∎

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be sextic
\mathbb{Z}/6
-del Pezzo surfaces with the same splitting field
\mathbf{F}
, parametrized by
(\rho,\xi)
and
(\rho^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
, respectively. Then
S\simeq S^{\prime}
if and only if
(\rho,\xi)\sim(\rho^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
where the equivalence relation is generated by:

(3.10)

(\rho,\xi)\sim(g(\rho),\xi),\,(\rho,\xi)\sim(\rho^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\,(\rho,\xi)\sim(\Norm_{h}(\lambda)\rho,\Norm_{g}(\lambda^{-1})\xi)\ \text{for}\ \lambda\in\mathbf{F}.

Proof.

Fix the twisted actions
\varphi\colon S_{\mathbf{F}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}}M
and
\varphi^{\prime}\colon S_{\mathbf{F}}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}}M
, and let
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\colon G\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\Aut_{\mathbf{F}}(M)
be the corresponding 1-cocylces. Then
S\simeq S^{\prime}
if and only if these cocycles are cohomologous, i.e. there is
\beta\in\Aut_{\mathbf{F}}(M)
such that

(3.11)

\alpha_{g}^{\prime}=\beta\alpha_{g}g(\beta^{-1}),\ \ \alpha_{h}^{\prime}=\beta\alpha_{h}h(\beta^{-1})

Thus, we start by looking at possible
\beta
s that keep the parametrization found in Lemma
3.7

\beta=((\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\mathrm{id})
:

((\xi^{\prime-1},\xi^{\prime-1}),\epsilon(g))=\alpha_{g}^{\prime}=\beta\alpha_{g}g(\beta^{-1})=\left(\left(\frac{\lambda_{1}g(\lambda_{2})}{\xi},\frac{\lambda_{2}g(\lambda_{2})}{g(\lambda_{1})\xi}\right),\epsilon(g)\right)

This is the case if and only if
\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{1}g(\lambda_{1})
and
\xi=\xi^{\prime}\Norm_{g}(\lambda_{1})
. Now for
\alpha_{h}
we find:

((\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}g(\rho^{\prime})),\epsilon(h))=\alpha_{h}^{\prime}=\beta\alpha_{h}h(\beta^{-1})=((\Norm_{h}(\lambda_{1})\rho,\Norm_{h}(\lambda_{2})\rho g(\rho)),\epsilon(h))

which is equivalent to
\rho^{\prime}=\Norm_{h}(\lambda_{1})\rho
.

\beta=((1,1),\epsilon(h))
:

\displaystyle((\xi^{\prime-1},\xi^{\prime-1}),\epsilon(g))=\alpha_{g}^{\prime}=\beta\alpha_{g}g(\beta^{-1})=((\xi,\xi),\epsilon(g))

\displaystyle((\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}g(\rho^{\prime})),\epsilon(h))=\alpha_{h}^{\prime}=\beta\alpha_{h}h(\beta^{-1})=((\rho^{-1},\rho^{-1}g(\rho^{-1})),\epsilon(h))

Therefore we get, that
\rho^{\prime}=\rho^{-1},\xi^{\prime}=\xi^{-1}
.

\beta=((1,1),\epsilon(g))
:

Put
\gamma=((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\mathrm{id})\circ\beta=((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(g))
. Then

\displaystyle\alpha_{g}^{\prime}=\gamma\alpha_{g}g(\gamma^{-1})=\alpha_{g},\ \ \ \ \alpha_{h}^{\prime}=\gamma\alpha_{h}h(\gamma^{-1})=((g^{2}(\rho),g^{2}(\rho)\rho),\epsilon(h)),

where we used that
\Norm_{h}(\xi)\Norm_{g}(\rho)=1
. This leads us to
(\rho^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})=(g^{2}(\rho),\xi)
, but this is the same as the first equivalence in (
3.10
).

Now take a general
\beta=((\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\tau)=((\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\mathrm{id})\circ((1,1),\tau)
,
\tau\in\mathrm{D}_{6}
, that gives an equivalence of 1-cycles. The equations of (
3.11
) imply that
\tau
belongs to the centralizer of
\epsilon(g)
in
\mathrm{D}_{6}
, hence
\tau\in\langle\epsilon(g),\epsilon(h)\rangle\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6
. Therefore,
\beta
is a composition of one of the maps from above, which finishes the proof.
∎

Recall that we assume
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{F}^{g}
and
\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{F}^{h}
in our Severi-Brauer data of
S
. By definition,
S
is
\mathbf{K}
-birational to a Severi-Brauer surface
X
and
\mathbf{L}
-birational to an involution surface
Y
, but the choice of these surfaces is not unique. However, we are able to list all possibilities for them.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
S
be a sextic
\mathbb{Z}/6
-del Pezzo surface, parametrized as in Proposition
3.7
. Then one has the following:

(1)

The Severi-Brauer surfaces over
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{F}^{g}
given by
S
are parametrized by
\xi
and
\xi^{-1}
.

(2)

The involution surfaces over
\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{F}^{h}
given by
S
are
C_{\rho}\times C_{g(\rho)}
,
C_{\rho}\times C_{g^{2}(\rho)}
and
C_{g(\rho)}\times C_{g^{2}(\rho)}
.

(3)

Moreover, the conics
C_{\rho}
,
C_{g(\rho)}
and
C_{g^{2}(\rho)}
are either all isomorphic to each other, or pairwise non-isomorphic and each of them is the Brauer product of the other two.

Proof.

(1)
Recall that the projection to the first factor in

M=\big\{([x_{0}:x_{1}:x_{2}],[y_{0}:y_{1}:y_{2}])\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\mathbf{F}}\times\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\mathbf{F}}:\ x_{0}y_{0}=x_{1}y_{1}=x_{2}y_{2}\big\}.

realizes
M
as the standard blow-up of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{F}}^{2}
. Since by Proposition
3.7
the twisted action is given by the automorphism

\alpha_{g}\colon([x_{0}:x_{1}:x_{2}],[y_{0}:y_{1}:y_{2}])\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.52 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.25pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.46 L 0 -3.46}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,([x_{2}:\xi^{-1}x_{0}:\xi^{-1}x_{1}],[y_{2}:\xi y_{0}:\xi y_{1}]),

we see, by projecting to the first and to the second factors, that the twisted actions on a Severi-Brauer surfaces are given, respectively, by the matrices

\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
\xi^{-1}&0&0\\
0&\xi^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}\sim\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\xi\\
1&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
\xi&0&0\\
0&\xi&0\end{pmatrix}\sim\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\xi^{-1}\\
1&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix},

and we are done by Lemma
2.8
.

(2)
We consider 3 rational maps
M\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{F}}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{F}}
which define the contractions of the 2-sections in 3 conic bundles on
M
, namely:

(3.12)

\displaystyle\pi_{01}\colon([x_{0}:x_{1}:x_{2}],[y_{0}:y_{1}:y_{2}])\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
