\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\begin{cases}([x_{0}:x_{2}],[y_{1}:y_{2}]),\quad y_{1}x_{0}\neq 0\\
([y_{2}:y_{0}],[x_{2}:x_{1}]),\quad x_{1}y_{0}\neq 0\end{cases}\ \text{contracts}\ x_{0}=x_{1}=y_{0}=y_{1}=0

(3.13)

\displaystyle\pi_{12}\colon([x_{0}:x_{1}:x_{2}],[y_{0}:y_{1}:y_{2}])\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.52 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.25pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.46 L 0 -3.46}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope

([y_{1}:y_{0}],[x_{2}:x_{0}]),\quad x_{2}y_{1}\neq 0\end{cases}\ \text{contracts}\ x_{1}=x_{2}=y_{1}=y_{2}=0

(3.14)

\displaystyle\pi_{02}\colon([x_{0}:x_{1}:x_{2}],[y_{0}:y_{1}:y_{2}])\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.52 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.25pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.46 L 0 -3.46}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\begin{cases}([x_{0}:x_{1}],[y_{1}:y_{2}]),\quad x_{0}y_{2}\neq 0\\
([y_{1}:y_{0}],[x_{2}:x_{1}]),\quad x_{2}y_{0}\neq 0\end{cases}\ \text{contracts}\ x_{0}=x_{2}=y_{0}=y_{2}=0

For the map
\pi_{01}
, we let
([u_{0}:u_{1}],[v_{0}:v_{1}])=([x_{0}:x_{2}],[y_{1}:y_{2}])
be the coordinates on
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{F}}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{F}}^{1}
, and find that the induced twisted action is

([u_{0}:u_{1}],[v_{0}:v_{1}])\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.52 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.25pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.46 L 0 -3.46}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,([u_{1}:\rho g(\rho)u_{0}],[v_{1}:g(\rho^{-1})v_{0}])=([g^{2}(\rho)\Norm_{h}(\xi^{-1})u_{1}:u_{0}],[g(\rho)v_{1}:v_{0}]),

so the surface is isomorphic to
C_{g^{2}(\rho)}\times C_{g(\rho)}
. We leave to the reader to write down the other two contractions and verify the induced twisted action. Similarly, for
\pi_{12}
and
\pi_{02}
we find that the induced twisted action defines the surfaces
C_{\rho}\times C_{g^{2}(\rho)}
and
C_{\rho}\times C_{g(\rho)}
, respectively.

(3)
If any element among
\{\rho,g(\rho),g^{2}(\rho)\}
is an
h
-norm, then the other two are also
h
-norms, since
h
and
g
commute. Thus, if any of these conics is
\mathbf{L}
-trivial, then the other two are also
\mathbf{L}
-trivial. Now suppose that two of these conics are isomorphic, say
\rho=\Norm_{h}(\lambda)g(\rho)
. Then
g(\rho)=\Norm_{h}(g(\lambda))g^{2}(\rho)
, and hence all 3 conics are isomorphic. Finally, if all 3 conics are pairwise non-isomorphic, then the claim follows from Theorem
2.13
applied to
\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{L}
.
∎

Theorem 3.10
.

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be two sextic
\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
-del Pezzo surfaces. Then
S\simeq S^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}^{\prime}
and their Severi-Brauer data are equivalent.

Proof.

If
S\simeq S^{\prime}
then
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}^{\prime}
and therefore
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
. Since
S_{\mathbf{K}}
is
\mathbf{K}
-isomorphic to
S_{\mathbf{K}}^{\prime}
, we find that
X
is
\mathbf{K}
-birational to
X^{\prime}
. Similarly,
Y
is
\mathbf{L}
-birational to
Y^{\prime}
. Conversely, suppose that
X
is
\mathbf{K}
-birational to
X^{\prime}
and
Y
is
\mathbf{L}
-birational to
Y^{\prime}
. Further, suppose that
S
and
S^{\prime}
are parametrized by the pairs
(\rho,\xi)
and
(\rho^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
, respectively.
We then need to show that these pairs are equivalent in the sense of Proposition
3.8
.

By Proposition
3.9
, one has
X\simeq X_{\xi}
or
X\simeq X_{\xi^{-1}}
. Similarly,
X^{\prime}\simeq X_{\xi^{\prime}}
or
X^{\prime}\simeq X_{{\xi^{\prime}}^{-1}}
. Since
X
is
\mathbf{K}
-birational to
X^{\prime}
, we get that
\xi=\xi^{\prime}
or
\xi={{\xi^{\prime}}^{-1}}
in
\mathbf{K}^{*}/\Norm_{g}(\mathbf{F}^{*})
, see Lemma
2.8
and Proposition
2.6
. By using the equivalences of Proposition
3.8
, we can replace the pair
(\rho,\xi)
with
(\rho^{-1},\xi^{-1})
, and hence from the very beginning assume that
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
parametrize isomorphic Severi-Brauer surfaces; note that
\rho
and
\rho^{-1}
parametrize isomorphic curves. Further, by Proposition
3.9
one has
Y\simeq C_{g^{i}(\rho)}\times C_{g^{j}(\rho)}
, where
i,j\in\{0,1,2\}
and
i\neq j
. Similarly,
Y^{\prime}\simeq C_{g^{k}(\rho^{\prime})}\times C_{g^{l}(\rho^{\prime})}
, where
k,l\in\{0,1,2\}
and
k\neq l
. But since
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
are
\mathbf{L}
-birational, Theorem
2.13
implies that
\rho^{\prime}=g^{t}(\rho)
in
\mathbf{L}^{*}/\Norm_{h}(\mathbf{F}^{*})
for
t\in\{0,1,2\}
.

So, by using the equivalences of Proposition
3.8
, we can switch, if necessary, between
\rho^{\prime}
,
g(\rho^{\prime})
, and
g^{2}(\rho^{\prime})
to make sure that
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
parametrize the same curve. This gives us
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathbf{F}
such that
\xi^{\prime}=\Norm_{g}(\lambda)\xi
and
\rho^{\prime}=\Norm_{h}(\lambda^{\prime})\rho
. Using the last equivalence of (
3.10
), we may assume that
\lambda^{\prime}=1
and hence
\rho^{\prime}=\rho
. Thus we need to show that
(\rho,\xi)\sim(\rho,\Norm_{g}(\lambda)\xi)
. The equalities
\Norm_{h}(\xi^{-1})=\Norm_{g}(\rho)
and
\Norm_{h}(\xi^{\prime-1})=\Norm_{g}(\rho^{\prime})
imply

\Norm_{g}(\rho)=\Norm_{h}(\xi^{-1})=\Norm_{h}\left({\xi^{\prime}}^{-1}\Norm_{g}(\lambda)\right)=\Norm_{g}(\rho^{\prime})\Norm_{h}(\Norm_{g}(\lambda)),

hence
\Norm_{h}(\Norm_{g}(\lambda))=1
. Define
\mu=\lambda h(\lambda^{-1})\Norm_{g}(\lambda^{-1})
and observe that
\Norm_{h}(\mu)=1
. Since
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=\Norm_{g}(h(\lambda)^{-1})
, we get
\Norm_{g}(\mu)=\Norm_{g}(\lambda^{-1})
. Using the last equivalence of (
3.10
) with coefficient
\mu^{-1}
, we obtain the desired equivalence.
∎

3.4.
Sextic
\Sym_{3}
-del Pezzo surfaces

The group
G\simeq\Sym_{3}
is generated by
\epsilon(g)
and
\epsilon(f)
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
S
be a sextic
\Sym_{3}
-del Pezzo surface. Then the twisted action (
2.2
) can be chosen so that

\alpha_{g}=((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(g)),\,\alpha_{f}=((\eta,f(\eta)),\epsilon(f))

for
\eta\in\mathbf{F}^{*}
, and

(3.15)

\xi=\frac{\eta}{f(\eta)fg(\eta)}\in\mathbf{F}^{g}.

Moreover every
\eta\in F^{*}
such that
\frac{\eta}{f(\eta)fg(\eta)}\in\mathbf{F}^{g}
yields a del Pezzo surface with said properties.

Proof.

We start with the same
\xi\in(\mathbf{F}^{*})^{g}
,
\alpha_{g}
that we found in the
\mathbb{Z}/6
-case. Let
\alpha_{f}=((\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\epsilon(f))
. We again use relations to extract the information about coefficients.

Firstly,
f^{2}=\mathrm{id}
, hence

((1,1),\mathrm{id})=\alpha_{f}f(\alpha_{f})=\left(\left(\frac{\lambda_{1}}{f(\lambda_{2})},\frac{\lambda_{2}}{f(\lambda_{1})}\right),\mathrm{id}\right).

Put
\eta=\lambda_{1}
, then
\lambda_{2}=f(\eta)
.

Secondly,
fg=g^{2}f
. Then
\alpha_{f}f(\alpha_{g})=\alpha_{g^{2}}g^{2}(\alpha_{f})
, which gives

\left(\left(\eta f(\xi),f(\eta\xi)\right),\epsilon(fg)\right)=\left(\left(g^{2}(f(\eta)\eta^{-1}),g^{2}(\eta^{-1})\xi^{-1}\right),\epsilon(g^{2}f)\right).

This is equivalent to

(3.16)

\Norm_{f}(g(\eta))=\Norm_{gf}(\eta),\ \ \xi=\frac{\eta}{f(\eta)fg(\eta)}.

It remains to notice that, adding to these two conditions the requirement
\xi\in\mathbf{F}^{g}
is equivalent to the condition (
3.15
).
∎

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be
\Sym_{3}
-del Pezzo surfaces of degree
6
, parametrized by
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
, respectively. Then
S\simeq S^{\prime}
if and only if
\eta\sim\eta^{\prime}
, where the equivalence relation is generated by
5
5

5

This implies
(\xi,\eta)\sim(\Norm_{g}(\lambda^{-1})\xi,\Norm_{f}(\lambda)fg(\lambda)\eta),\ (\xi,\eta)\sim(\xi^{-1},\eta^{-1}).
:

\eta\sim\Norm_{f}(\lambda)fg(\lambda)\eta,\ \ \ \eta\sim\eta^{-1}.

Proof.

As in the previous proof, we notice that if
\alpha_{g}
and
\alpha_{g}^{\prime}
are equivalent via the toric automorphism, it is of the form
\beta=((\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda)),\mathrm{id})
, where
\xi=\xi^{\prime}\Norm_{g}(\lambda)
. Now for
\alpha_{f}
we get

\alpha_{f}^{\prime}=((\eta^{\prime},f(\eta^{\prime})),\epsilon(f))=\beta\alpha_{f}f(\beta^{-1})=\left((\Norm_{f}(\lambda)\eta fg(\lambda),\Norm_{f}(\lambda)g(\lambda)f(\eta)),\epsilon(f)\right),

which gives
\eta^{\prime}=\Norm_{f}(\lambda)fg(\lambda)\eta
.

Further, equivalence via
\beta=((1,1),\epsilon(h))
gives us
\eta^{\prime}=\eta^{-1}
. (Here and below, by abuse of notation, we write
\epsilon(h)
to denote the central involution of
\mathrm{D}_{6}
, although
G
does not contain
h
.) Since the centralizer of
\langle\epsilon(g),\epsilon(f)\rangle
in
\mathrm{D}_{6}
is
\epsilon(h)
, we are done.
∎

We now get a better reparametrization.

Proposition 3.13
.

In the setting of Lemma
3.11
, the twisted action
\varphi
can be chosen so that

\alpha_{g}=((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(g)),\ \ \ \alpha_{f}=((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(f)),

where
\xi\in\mathbf{k}^{*}
. Two such parametrizations yield the same surface if and only if
\xi\sim\xi^{\prime}
where the equivalence is generated by
\xi\sim\xi^{-1},\,\xi\sim\Norm_{g}(\mu)\xi
, with
\mu\in{(\mathbf{F}^{*})}^{f}

Proof.

We use equivalence found in Proposition
3.12
with coefficient
\lambda=fg(\eta^{-1})
to repalce
\eta
by
\Norm_{f}(fg(\eta^{-1})))fg(fg(\eta^{-1})))\eta=\Norm_{f}(g(\eta^{-1}))
. Hence we can assume that
\eta\in\mathbf{F}^{f}
. But now the first equality in (
3.16
) implies
\eta\in\mathbf{F}^{g}
. So,
\eta\in\mathbf{k}^{*}
. Then the second equality in (
3.16
) implies
\eta=\xi^{-1}
.

The equivalence via the automorphism
\beta=((1,1),\epsilon(h))
gives that
(\xi,\xi)\sim(\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1})
. If
\beta=((\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda)),\mathrm{id})
, then

\alpha_{g}^{\prime}=((\xi^{\prime-1},\xi^{\prime-1}),\epsilon(g))=\beta\alpha_{g}g(\beta^{-1})

implies
\xi^{\prime}=\xi\Norm_{g}(\lambda^{-1})
, as in the proof of Proposition
3.12
. On the other hand,

\alpha_{f}^{\prime}=(({\xi^{\prime}}^{-1},{\xi^{\prime}}^{-1}),\epsilon(f))=\beta\alpha_{f}f(\beta^{-1})=((\lambda f(\lambda)fg(\lambda)\xi^{-1},\lambda g(\lambda)f(\lambda)\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(f)).

Then
\lambda f(\lambda)fg(\lambda)\xi^{-1}=\lambda g(\lambda)f(\lambda)\xi^{-1}
, so
fg(\lambda)=g(\lambda)
. On the other hand, since
\lambda g(\lambda)f(\lambda)\xi^{-1}
must be
f,g
-invariant, we get
\lambda=gf(\lambda)
. So,
\lambda g(\lambda)f(\lambda)=\lambda g(\lambda)g^{2}(\lambda)=\Norm_{g}(\lambda)
, hence

\alpha_{f}^{\prime}=(({\xi^{\prime}}^{-1},{\xi^{\prime}}^{-1}),\epsilon(f))=\beta\alpha_{f}f(\beta^{-1})=((\Norm_{g}(\lambda)\xi^{-1},\Norm_{g}(\lambda)\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(f)),

i.e. we again get
\xi^{\prime}=\Norm_{g}(\lambda^{-1})\xi
. Putting
\mu=g(\lambda^{-1})
, we get the result.
∎

Theorem 3.14
.

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be two sextic
\Sym_{3}
-del Pezzo surfaces. Then
S\simeq S^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}^{\prime}
and their Severi-Brauer data are equivalent.

Proof.

The necessity is clear. Suppose that
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
parametrize
S
and
S^{\prime}
, respectively. If the corresponding Severi-Brauer surfaces
X
and
X^{\prime}
are
\mathbf{K}
-birational, then
\xi^{\prime}=\xi
or
\xi^{\prime}=\xi^{-1}
in
\mathbf{K}^{*}/\Norm_{g}(\mathbf{F}^{*})
and Proposition
3.13
implies that we can replace
\xi
with
\xi^{-1}
, if needed, and assume that
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
parametrize isomorphic Severi-Brauer surfaces, thus
\xi^{\prime}=\Norm_{g}(\mu^{\prime})\xi
. Since both
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
are fixed by
f
, we see that
\Norm_{g}(\mu^{\prime})
is fixed by
f
. Then

\mu=\frac{\Norm_{g}(\mu^{\prime})}{\mu^{\prime}f(\mu^{\prime})}

is fixed by
f
and has the same
\Norm_{g}
-norm as
\mu^{\prime}
. This gives that the two sextic del Pezzos are isomorphic.
∎

3.5.
Sextic
\mathrm{D}_{6}
-del Pezzo surfaces

By abuse of notation, denoting
\epsilon(g)
,
\epsilon(f)
and
\epsilon(h)
by
g
,
f
and
h
, respectively, we have

\mathrm{D}_{6}=\langle g,f,h\ |\ g^{3}=f^{2}=h^{2}=\mathrm{id},\ hf=fh,\ gh=hg,\ fgf=g^{2}\rangle.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
S
be a sextic
\mathrm{D}_{6}
-del Pezzo surface. Then the twisted action (
2.2
) can be chosen so that

\alpha_{g}=((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(g)),\ \ \alpha_{f}=((\eta,f(\eta)),\epsilon(f)),\ \ \alpha_{h}=((\rho,\rho g(\rho)),\epsilon(h))

with conditions

(3.17)

\xi=\frac{\eta}{f(\eta)fg(\eta)},\ \ \Norm_{h}(\xi)\Norm_{g}(\rho)=1,\ \ g(\rho)\Norm_{f}(\rho)=\Norm_{h}(f(\eta)),\ \ \xi\in(\mathbf{F}^{*})^{g},\ \ \rho\in(\mathbf{F}^{*})^{h}.

Moreover every such pair
(\rho,\eta)
yields a del Pezzo surface with said properties.

Proof.

By combining the proofs of Proposition
3.7
and Lemma
3.11
, we get the required parametrizations by
\xi
,
\eta
and
\rho
, satisfying the condition (
3.9
) and (
3.16
). The condition
\alpha_{hf}=\alpha_{fh}
is then equivalent to

(3.18)

g(\rho)\Norm_{f}(\rho)=\Norm_{h}(f(\eta)).

∎

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be sextic
\mathrm{D}_{6}
-del Pezzo surfaces, parametrized by
(\rho,\eta)
and
(\rho^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
, respectively. Then
S\simeq S^{\prime}
if and only if
(\rho,\eta)\sim(\rho^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
, where the equivalence relation is generated by

(\rho,\eta)\sim(\rho^{-1},\eta^{-1}),\ \ (\rho,\eta)\sim(\Norm_{h}(\lambda)\rho,\Norm_{f}(\lambda)fg(\lambda)\eta),\ \ \lambda\in\mathbf{F}^{*}.

Proof.

Indeed, if two 1-cocylces
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
are cohomologous via
\beta=((\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\tau)
, then

\tau\in\Centralizer_{\mathrm{D}_{6}}(\epsilon(g))\cap\Centralizer_{\mathrm{D}_{6}}(\epsilon(h))\cap\Centralizer_{\mathrm{D}_{6}}(\epsilon(f))=\langle\epsilon(h)\rangle.

Now the result follows from the proofs of Proposition
3.8
and
3.12
.
∎

As in the previous case, we are now able to get rid of some parameters:

Proposition 3.17
.

Let
S
be a sextic
\mathrm{D}_{6}
-del Pezzo surface. Then the twisted action (
2.2
) can be chosen so that

\alpha_{g}=((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(g)),\ \ \alpha_{f}=((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(f)),\ \ \alpha_{h}=((\rho,\rho g(\rho)),\epsilon(h))

with conditions

(3.19)

\Norm_{h}(\xi)\Norm_{g}(\rho)=1,\ \ g(\rho)\Norm_{f}(\rho)\Norm_{h}(\xi)=1,\ \ \xi\in(\mathbf{F}^{*})^{\langle g,f\rangle},\ \ \rho\in(\mathbf{F}^{*})^{h}.

Moreover every such pair
(\rho,\xi)
yields a del Pezzo surface with said properties.

Two such parametrizations
(\rho,\xi)
and
(\rho^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
yield the same surface if and only if
(\rho,\xi)\sim(\rho^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
where the equivalence is generated by

(\rho,\xi)\sim(\rho^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\ \ (\rho,\xi)\sim(\Norm_{h}(\mu)\rho,\Norm_{g}(\mu^{-1})\xi),\ \ \mu\in(\mathbf{F}^{*})^{gf}.

Proof.

We again use the second equivalence found in Proposition
3.16
with coefficient
\lambda=fg(\eta^{-1})
to repalce
\eta
by
\Norm_{f}(fg(\eta^{-1})))fg(fg(\eta^{-1})))\eta=\Norm_{f}(g(\eta^{-1}))
to assume that
\eta\in\mathbf{F}^{f}
. The first and the fourth equalities in (
3.17
) imply

\eta=\frac{g(\eta)fg(\eta)}{gf(\eta)}=\frac{g(\eta)g^{2}(\eta)}{g(\eta)}=g^{2}(\eta),

so
\eta\in\mathbf{F}^{g}
. Now the first equality in (
3.17
) gives
\eta=\xi^{-1}
.

The equivalence via the automorphism
\beta=((1,1),\epsilon(h))
translates to
(\rho,\xi)\sim(\rho^{-1},\xi^{-1})
, as in the proof of Proposition
3.8
. The equivalence via a toric automorphism, necessarily of the form
\beta=((\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda)),\mathrm{id})
, translates to
(\rho,\xi)\sim(\Norm_{h}(\mu)\rho,\Norm_{g}(\mu^{-1})\xi)
, as in the proof of Propositions
3.8
and
3.13
, where
\mu
is fixed by
gf
.
∎

Proposition 3.18
.

Let
S
be a sextic
\mathrm{D}_{6}
-del Pezzo surface, parametrized as in Proposition
3.17
. Then one has the following:

(1)

The Severi-Brauer surfaces over
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,s\rangle}
given by
S
are parametrized by
\xi
and
\xi^{-1}
.

(2)

The involution surfaces over
\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{F}^{h}
given by
S
are
C_{\rho}\times C_{g(\rho)}
,
C_{\rho}\times C_{g^{2}(\rho)}
and
C_{g(\rho)}\times C_{g^{2}(\rho)}
.

(3)

Moreover, the conics
C_{\rho}
,
C_{g(\rho)}
and
C_{g^{2}(\rho)}
are either all isomorphic to each other, or pairwise non-isomorphic and each of them is the Brauer product of the other two.

Proof.

We only prove the statement
(1)
about surfaces, because for involution surfaces and curves the calculation is the same as in Proposition
3.9
. We use the the parametrization found in Proposition
3.17
. Note that the contraction of the 3 curves
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
and
E_{3}
is now defined over a field
\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,s\rangle}
. We have

\alpha_{s}=\alpha_{h}h(\alpha_{f})=((\rho h(\xi),\rho g(\rho)h(\xi)),\epsilon(hf)),

hence the induced twisted action of
\alpha_{g}
and
\alpha_{s}
after contraction is given by the matrices

\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\xi\\
1&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix}\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&\rho h(\xi)\\
0&\rho g(\rho)h(\xi)&0\end{pmatrix}.

Notice that

\rho g(\rho)h(\xi)=s(\rho^{-1}h(\xi^{-1})),

because this is equivalent (in view of
\rho\in\mathbf{F}^{h}
and
\xi\in\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,f\rangle}
) to
\Norm_{f}(\rho)\Norm_{h}(\xi)g(\rho)=1
, the equality insured by
3.19
. On the other hand, computing
\tau s(\tau)sg(\tau)
with
\tau=\rho h(\xi)
gives
\xi
. We conclude by using Lemma
2.9
.
∎

Theorem 3.19
.

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be two sextic
\mathrm{D}_{6}
-del Pezzo surfaces. Then
S\simeq S^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}^{\prime}
and their Severi-Brauer data are equivalent.

Proof.

The necessity is obvious, so we prove the sufficiency. Suppose that
S
and
S^{\prime}
are parametrized by the pairs
(\rho,\xi)
and
(\rho^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
, respectively. We then show that these pairs are equivalent in the sense of Proposition
3.17
. In what follows we use that

g(\rho)\Norm_{f}(\rho)\Norm_{h}(\xi)=\Norm_{g}(\rho)\Norm_{h}(\xi)=1

implies
\rho=gf(\rho)
.

By Proposition
3.18
, one has
X\simeq X_{\xi}
or
X\simeq X_{\xi^{-1}}
, and
X^{\prime}\simeq X_{\xi^{\prime}}
or
X^{\prime}\simeq X_{{\xi^{\prime}}^{-1}}
. Since
X
is
\mathbf{K}
-birational to
X^{\prime}
, we get that
\xi=\xi^{\prime}
or
\xi={{\xi^{\prime}}^{-1}}
in
\mathbf{K}^{*}/\Norm_{g}(\mathbf{F}^{*})
. By using the equivalences of Proposition
3.17
, we can replace the pair
(\rho,\xi)
with
(\rho^{-1},\xi^{-1})
, and hence assume that
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
parametrize isomorphic Severi-Brauer surfaces. As in the proof of Theorem
3.10
, we get
\rho^{\prime}=g^{t}(\rho)
in
\mathbf{L}^{*}/\Norm_{h}(\mathbf{F}^{*})
for
t\in\{0,1,2\}
.

If
\rho^{\prime}
parametrizes the same curve as
g(\rho)
we find that
\rho^{\prime}=g(\rho)\Norm_{h}(\lambda)
, where
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}
. Since
\rho^{\prime}
and
\rho
are fixed by
gs=sg^{2}
, we get that

s(\rho)\Norm_{h}(sg^{2}(\lambda))=g(\rho)\Norm_{h}(\lambda),

hence, by applying
s
to both sides, we have

g(\rho)=\rho\Norm_{h}\left(\frac{g^{2}(\lambda)}{s(\lambda)}\right).

Similarly, if
\rho^{\prime}
parametrizes the same curve as
g^{2}(\rho)
we get that
\rho^{\prime}=g^{2}(\rho)\Norm_{h}(\lambda)
. Since
\rho^{\prime}
is
sg^{2}
-invariant, we get
sg(\rho)\Norm_{h}(sg^{2}(\lambda))=g^{2}(\rho)\Norm_{h}(\lambda)
. Applying
sg
to this yields
\rho\Norm_{h}(g(\lambda))=s(\rho)\Norm_{h}(sg(\lambda))
, and by using
sg^{2}(\rho)=\rho
we obtain that
\rho\Norm_{h}(g(\lambda))=s(sg^{2}(\rho))\Norm_{h}(sg(\lambda))=g^{2}(\rho)\Norm_{h}(sg(\lambda))
. Hence

g^{2}(\rho)=\rho N_{h}\left(\frac{g(\lambda)}{sg(\lambda)}\right).

Thus, we may assume that
\rho^{\prime}
parametrizes the same conic as
\rho
. Then there are
\delta,\theta\in\mathbf{F}
such that
\rho^{\prime}=\Norm_{h}(\delta)\rho
and
\xi^{\prime}=\Norm_{g}(\theta)\xi
. Since
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\mathbf{F}^{f}
we find that
\Norm_{g}(f(\theta))=f(\Norm_{g}(\theta))=\Norm_{g}(\theta)
. Let
\theta^{\prime}=\Norm_{g}(\theta)/\theta gf(\theta)
, then
\theta^{\prime}\in(\mathbf{F}^{gf})^{*}
and
\Norm_{g}(\theta^{\prime})=\Norm_{g}(\theta)
, so we can use a norm-equivalence with
\mu=\theta^{\prime}
to get

(\rho^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})=(\Norm_{h}(\delta)\rho,\Norm_{g}(\theta)\xi)\sim(\Norm_{h}(\theta^{\prime})\Norm_{h}(\delta)\rho,\Norm_{g}({\theta^{\prime}}^{-1})\Norm_{g}(\theta)\xi)=(\Norm_{h}(\theta^{\prime}\delta)\rho,\xi)

and hence assume
\xi^{\prime}=\xi
from the very beginning. So, it remains to show that the pair
(\Norm_{h}(\delta)\rho,\xi)
is equivalent to
(\rho,\xi)
. Note that the conditions (
3.19
) imply

\Norm_{g}(\Norm_{h}(\delta))=1,\ \ g\Norm_{h}(\delta)\Norm_{f}(\Norm_{h}(\delta))=1.

We claim that there exists
\zeta\in\mathbf{F}^{gf}
such that
\Norm_{g}(\zeta)=1
,
\Norm_{h}(\zeta)=\Norm_{h}(\delta)
. This will achieve the proof by using the norm-equivalence of Proposition
3.17
.

We can write every
u\in\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})
as
h^{i}v
, where
v\in\langle g,f\rangle,i\in\{0,1\}
. It is easy to check that setting

\alpha_{u}=((\Norm_{h}(\delta^{i}),\Norm_{h}(\delta^{i}g(\delta^{i}))),\epsilon(u)).

defines a sextic
\mathrm{D}_{6}
-surface
S_{0}
parametrized by
\rho_{0}=\Norm_{h}(\delta)
,
\xi_{0}=1
. In particular,

\alpha_{g}=((1,1),\epsilon(g)),\ \ \alpha_{h}=((\Norm_{h}(\delta),\Norm_{h}(\delta g(\delta)),\epsilon(h)),\ \ \alpha_{f}=((1,1),\epsilon(f)).

A pair
(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
corresponds to a
\mathbf{k}
-rational point on
S_{0}
if and only if
\alpha_{u}\circ u(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})=(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
for
u=g,h,f
. For
u=g
we get

(3.20)

\displaystyle(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})=\alpha_{g}(g(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}))=(g(\zeta_{2}^{-1}),g(\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}^{-1}))

which is equivalent to
\zeta_{2}=\zeta_{1}g(\zeta_{1})
,
\Norm_{g}(\zeta_{1})=1
.

For
u=h
we get

(3.21)

\displaystyle(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})=\alpha_{h}(h(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}))=(\Norm_{h}(\delta)h(\zeta_{1}^{-1}),\Norm_{h}(\delta g(\delta))h(b\zeta 2^{-1}))

which is equivalent to
\Norm_{h}(\zeta_{1})=\Norm_{h}(\delta)
,
\Norm_{h}(\zeta_{2})=\Norm_{h}(\delta g(\delta))
.

For
u=f
we get

(3.22)

\displaystyle(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})=\alpha_{f}(f(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}))=(f(\zeta_{2}^{-1}),f(\zeta_{1}^{-1}))

which is equivalent to
\zeta_{2}=f(\zeta_{1})^{-1}
. Thus, the existence of a
\mathbf{k}
-rational point on
S_{0}
is equivalent to the existence of
\zeta\in\mathbf{F}^{*}
such that
\Norm_{g}(\zeta)=1
,
\zeta g(\zeta)f(\zeta)=1
,
\Norm_{h}(\zeta)=\Norm_{h}(\delta)
which is further equivalent to
\Norm_{g}(\zeta)=1
,
\Norm_{h}(\zeta)=\Norm_{h}(\delta)
,
gf(\zeta)=\zeta
. This is exactly
\zeta
we are looking for. It remains to notice that
S_{0}
is
\mathbf{k}
-rational. This can be proven using the results of Section
5
, but we sketch a direct argument below.

Let us prove that there is a
2
-point and a
3
-point on
S_{0}
. Then we can take the two
6
-curves that intersect all the lines of the hexagon ones all the components of the
3
-point ones and one of the components of the
2
-points twice and one component once (they are the strict transform of the geometric cubic plane curves through all eight points with a double point at one of the components of a degree 2 point). Their intersection multiplicity is then
6
but they intersect each other in only
5
points with multiplicity
1
and therefore there is a
6
th point. Since those two curves form a Galois orbit, this point must be
\mathbf{k}
-rational.

Points of degree 2 and 3 can be found directly by taking suitable orbits under the actions (
3.20
), (
3.21
) and (
3.22
). The point
(1,1)
is invariant under
\alpha_{g}\circ g
and
\alpha_{f}\circ f
, hence its
\mathrm{D}_{6}
-orbit is
\{(1,1),(\Norm_{h}(\delta),\Norm_{h}(\delta g(\delta)))\}
, which gives a point of degree 2 on
S_{0}
. The point
(\delta,f(\delta^{-1}))
is obviously
\alpha_{f}\circ f
-invariant, and

\{(\delta,f(\delta^{-1})),(g(f(\delta)),g(af(\delta))),(g^{2}(f(\delta^{-1})\delta^{-1}),g^{2}(\delta^{-1}))\}

is its
\mathrm{D}_{6}
-orbit, giving a point of degree 3 on
S_{0}
. Indeed, the
\alpha_{g}\circ g
-invariance of this set is straightforward. As was noticed in the beginning of the proof,
\rho_{0}=\Norm_{h}(\delta)
is
gf=fg^{2}
-invariant. We hence get

\alpha_{h}\circ h(\delta,f(\delta^{-1}))=(\Norm_{h}(\delta)h(\delta^{-1}),g^{2}\Norm_{h}(\delta^{-1})hf(\delta))=(\delta,ffg^{2}\Norm_{h}(\delta^{-1})hf(\delta))=(\delta,f(\delta^{-1})),

hence
\alpha_{h}\circ h
fixes each element of the set, as
h
commutes with
g
. Finally, we observe that

\alpha_{f}\circ f(gf(\delta),g(af(\delta)))=(fg(\delta^{-1}f(\delta^{-1})),fgf(\delta^{-1}))=(g^{2}(f(\delta^{-1})a^{-1}),g^{2}(\delta^{-1})).

∎

3.6.
The Amitsur invariant

As was already recalled in the Introduction, for any smooth projective geometrically irreducible variety
X
over
\mathbf{k}
, there is an exact sequence of groups

0\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\Pic(X)\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\Pic(X_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}})^{\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{k}}/\mathbf{k})}\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\Br(\mathbf{k})\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\Br(\mathbf{k}(X)).

The group
\Am(S)=\Pic(X_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}})^{\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{k}}/\mathbf{k})}/\Pic(X)\subset\Br(\mathbf{k})
is called the
Amitsur subgroup
of
X
in
\Br(\mathbf{k})
. Note that this is a birational invariant.

Example 3.20
.

By
[
CTKM08
, Proposition 5.1]
and
[
Tre23
, Proposition 2.5]
, one has the following.

(1)

If
S
is a Severi-Brauer surface over a field
\mathbf{k}
, then
\Am(S)
is spanned by the class of a central simple algebra of degree 3 over
\mathbf{k}
. In particular, this group is either trivial, or isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
, and the latter holds if and only if
S
(or the corresponding algebra) is not trivial.

(2)

Suppose that
S\simeq C_{1}\times C_{2}
, where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are two smooth conics. If
C_{1}\simeq C_{2}\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{k}}^{1}
, then
\Am(S)=0
. If
C_{1}\simeq C_{2}
are both not trivial, then
\Am(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2
is generated by the class of
C_{1}
in
\Br(\mathbf{k})[2]
, the 2-torsion subgroup of the Brauer group. If
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are not trivial and not isomorphic to each other, then
\Am(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2
is generated by the classes of
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
in
\Br(\mathbf{k})[2]
.

Proof of Theorem
A
.

The necessity is obvious, so we will prove that the three conditions from the statement are sufficient. The fields
\mathbf{K}
and
\mathbf{L}
are uniquely defined by
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}^{\prime}
,
S
and
S^{\prime}
as the fields from the Severi-Brauer data. By Example
3.20
, if
\Am(S_{\mathbf{K}})=\Am(S^{\prime}_{\mathbf{K}})=0
, then
X\simeq X^{\prime}\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{K}}^{2}
. If
\Am(S_{\mathbf{K}})=\Am(S^{\prime}_{\mathbf{K}})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
, then
X
and
X^{\prime}
both generate the same subgroup of
\Br(\mathbf{K})
isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
, hence are birational. Similarly, the groups
\Am(S_{\mathbf{L}})
and
\Am(S^{\prime}_{\mathbf{L}})
are both either trivial, or isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2
. In the fist case,
Y\simeq Y^{\prime}\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{L}}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{L}}^{1}
. In the second case, the non-trivial elements of the Amitsur subgroup correspond to 3 non-trivial conics of Propositions
3.9
and
3.18
.
∎

We leave to the reader the reformulation this biregular classification in terms of two Brauer elements
\alpha\in\Br(\mathbf{K})[3]
and
\beta\in\Br(\mathbf{L})[2]
, as in
[
Blu10
]
.

4.
Automorphism groups

In this Section, we use the parametrization of sextic
G
-del Pezzo surfaces found in Section
3
, to compute their automorphism groups. This complements the results of J. Schneider and S. Zimmermann
[
SZ21
]
who computed the automorphism groups of
\mathbf{k}
-rational surfaces, and also the works of C. Shramov and V. Vologodsky
[
SV18
]
, and C. Shramov and A. Vikulova
[
SV25
]
which deal with automorphism groups of del Pezzo surfaces without
\mathbf{k}
-rational points; see
[
Boi23
,
Smi25
,
Smi23
,
Zai23
,
Zai24
]
for related discussion.

Let
\varphi\colon S_{\mathbf{F}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}}M
be the twisted action (
2.2
). Consider the group homomorphism

\Phi\colon\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S)\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\Aut_{\mathbf{F}}(M),\ \ \gamma\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.52 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.25pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.46 L 0 -3.46}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\varphi\circ\gamma\circ\varphi^{-1}.

Note that, since
\gamma
commutes with all
u\in G
, one has

\alpha_{u}\circ u\circ\Phi(\gamma)=\varphi\circ u\circ\varphi^{-1}\circ\varphi\circ\gamma\circ\varphi^{-1}=\varphi\circ\gamma\circ u\circ\varphi^{-1}=\Phi(\gamma)\circ\alpha_{u}\circ u,

so
\Phi
induces an isomorphism

\Phi\colon\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}}\{\gamma\in\Aut_{\mathbf{F}}(M)\colon u(\gamma)=\alpha_{u}^{-1}\circ\gamma\circ\alpha_{u}\ \text{for all}\ u\in G\},

where the action of
u
on
\gamma
is given by the conjugation (
2.1
).

In the following theorem, we stick to notation used in Section
3
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
S
be a non-
\mathbf{k}
-rational sextic
G
-del Pezzo surface,
\mathbf{F}
be its splitting field and
\varphi:S_{\mathbf{F}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}}M
be the isomorphism (
2.2
).

(1)

Suppose that
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6
. Put

T_{1}=\big\{\big((\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda)),\mathrm{id}\big)\colon\lambda\in\mathbf{F}^{*},\ \Norm_{g}(\lambda)=\Norm_{h}(\lambda)=1\big\}\subset T_{\mathbf{F}}.

Then one of the following holds:

(a)

\Phi(\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S))\simeq T_{1}
if
\xi,\rho
are non trivial;

(b)

\Phi(\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S))\simeq T_{1}\rtimes\langle\alpha_{h}\rangle
if
\xi
is trivial;

(c)

\Phi(\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S))\simeq T_{1}\rtimes\langle\alpha_{g}\rangle
if
\rho
is trivial.

(2)

Suppose that
G\simeq\Sym_{3}
. Put

T_{2}=\big\{\big((\lambda,f(\lambda^{-1})),\mathrm{id}\big)\colon\lambda\in({\mathbf{F}^{*}})^{gf},\ \Norm_{g}(\lambda)=1\big\}\subset T_{\mathbf{F}}.

Then
\Phi(\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S))\simeq T_{2}
(note that
\xi
is not trivial here).

(3)

Suppose that
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
. Put

T_{3}=\big\{\big((\lambda,f(\lambda^{-1})),\mathrm{id}\big)\colon\lambda\in({\mathbf{F}^{*}})^{gf},\ \Norm_{g}(\lambda)=\Norm_{h}(\lambda)=1\big\}\subset T_{\mathbf{F}}.

Then one of the following holds:

(a)

\Phi(\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S))\simeq T_{3}
if
\xi
is not trivial;

(b)

\Phi(\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S))\simeq T_{3}\rtimes\langle\alpha_{h}\rangle
if
\xi
is trivial.

Proof.

Take
\gamma=((\lambda,\mu),v)\in\Phi(\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S))
. Recall that we have the split short exact sequence (
3.4
) and the subgroup group
\mathrm{D}_{6}\subset\Aut(S_{\mathbf{F}})
is generated by the automorphisms
\iota
,
\theta
and
\sigma
, thus
v\circ u=u\circ v
for all
u\in G
. Therefore,
v\in\langle\epsilon(g),\epsilon(h)\rangle
if
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6
, and
v\in\langle\epsilon(h)\rangle
if
G\simeq\Sym_{3}
or
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
. We then consider 3 cases.

(1)
Suppose that
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6
. We first show that if
v
is of order 3, then
S
is
\mathbf{L}
-trivial, i.e.
\rho
is an
h
-Norm. Indeed, in this case we may assume that
v=\epsilon(g)
and then the condition
u(\gamma)=\alpha_{u}^{-1}\circ\gamma\circ\alpha_{u}
translates, for
u=g
and
u=h
respectively, to

(4.1)

\displaystyle((1,\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(g^{-1}))\circ((\lambda,\mu),\epsilon(g))\circ((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(g))=((\xi^{-1}\lambda^{-1}\mu,\xi^{-2}\lambda^{-1}),\epsilon(g))=((g(\lambda),g(\mu)),\mathrm{id})\hskip 28.45274pt

(4.2)

\displaystyle((\rho,\rho g(\rho)),\epsilon(h))\circ((\lambda,\mu),\epsilon(g))\circ((\rho,\rho g(\rho)),\epsilon(h))=((\lambda^{-1}\rho^{2}g(\rho),\mu^{-1}\rho g(\rho^{2})),\epsilon(g))=((h(\lambda),h(\mu)),\epsilon(g))\hskip 28.45274pt

which is equivalent to

(4.3)

\begin{cases}\mu=\lambda g(\lambda)\xi,\\
\lambda g(\mu)\xi^{2}=1\end{cases}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \begin{cases}\Norm_{h}(\lambda)=\rho^{2}g(\rho)\\
\Norm_{h}(\mu)=\rho g(\rho^{2}).\end{cases}

Since
\rho\in\mathbf{F}^{h}
, we get
\Norm_{h}(\lambda)=\Norm_{h}(\rho)g(\rho)
, so
\rho=\Norm_{h}(g^{2}(\lambda/\rho))
. Therefore, we may assume that
\rho=1
.

So, if
S
is not
\mathbf{L}
-rational, then
\Phi(\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S))
cannot contain elements
((\lambda,\mu),v)
with
v
or order 3 or 6, hence
v=\mathrm{id}
, or
v=\epsilon(h)
. In the first case, we get

(4.4)

\displaystyle((1,\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(g^{-1}))\circ((\lambda,\mu),\mathrm{id})\circ((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(g))=((\lambda^{-1}\mu,\lambda^{-1}),\mathrm{id})=((g(\lambda),g(\mu)),\mathrm{id})\ \ \text{for}\ u=g\hskip 28.45274pt

(4.5)

\displaystyle((\rho,\rho g(\rho)),\epsilon(h))\circ((\lambda,\mu),\mathrm{id})\circ((\rho,\rho g(\rho)),\epsilon(h))=((\lambda^{-1},\mu^{-1}),\mathrm{id})=((h(\lambda),h(\mu)),\mathrm{id})\ \ \text{for}\ u=h.\hskip 28.45274pt

The first equality is equivalent to
\mu=\lambda g(\lambda)
,
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=1
, while the second is equivalent to
\Norm_{h}(\lambda)=\Norm_{h}(\mu)=1
. We conclude that all toric elements are of the form

(4.6)

\big\{\big((\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda)),\mathrm{id}\big)\colon\lambda\in\mathbf{F}^{*},\ \Norm_{g}(\lambda)=\Norm_{h}(\lambda)=1\big\}.

In the second case
v=\epsilon(h)
we obtain

(4.7)

\displaystyle((1,\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(g^{-1}))\circ((\lambda,\mu),\epsilon(h))\circ((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(g))=((\lambda^{-1}\mu,\lambda^{-1}\xi^{-2}),\epsilon(h))=((g(\lambda),g(\mu)),\epsilon(h)),\hskip 28.45274pt

(4.8)

\displaystyle((\rho,\rho g(\rho)),\epsilon(h))\circ((\lambda,\mu),\epsilon(h))\circ((\rho,\rho g(\rho)),\epsilon(h))=((\lambda^{-1}\rho^{2},\mu^{-1}\rho^{2}g(\rho^{2})),\epsilon(h))=((h(\lambda),h(\mu)),\epsilon(h)).\hskip 28.45274pt

which is equivalent to

(4.9)

\begin{cases}\mu=\lambda g(\lambda),\\
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)\xi^{2}=1\end{cases}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \begin{cases}\Norm_{h}(\lambda)=\rho^{2}\\
\Norm_{h}(\mu)=\rho^{2}g(\rho^{2}).\end{cases}

Now
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)\xi^{2}=1
is equivalent (recall that
\xi\in\mathbf{F}^{g}
) to
\Norm_{g}(\lambda\xi)=\xi
, hence
S
is
\mathbf{K}
-trivial.
We conclude that if
S
is neither
\mathbf{K}
-trivial, nor
\mathbf{L}
-trivial, then
\Phi(\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S))
consists only of toric automorphisms. If
S
is
\mathbf{K}
-trivial, then we put
\xi=1
. Then
\alpha_{h}\in\Phi(\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S))
and together with toric automorphisms from above generates the whole group
\Phi(\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S))
. As was shown above, it acts on toric automorphism by inversion.

Finally, suppose that
S
is
\mathbf{L}
-trivial, i.e.
\rho=1
. Note that
S
is not
\mathbf{K}
-trivial, since we assume
S
not
\mathbf{k}
-rational. Therefore,
v
cannot be of order 2 (and hence of order 6 either), since otherwise (
4.7
), (
4.8
) and (
4.9
) again imply that
\xi=\Norm_{g}(\lambda\xi)
, a contradiction. As before, we find that toric automorphisms are of the form (
4.6
), so it remains to determine the form of an automorphism with
v
of order 3. But here we simply notice, using
\Norm_{h}(\xi)=1
, that
\alpha_{g}\in\Phi(\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S))
, hence the result.

(2)
Here every automorphism in
\Phi(\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S))
is of the form
\gamma=((\lambda,\mu),\mathrm{id})
, or
\gamma=((\lambda,\mu),\epsilon(h))
. In the first case, the condition
u(\gamma)=\alpha_{u}^{-1}\circ\gamma\circ\alpha_{u}
translates to

(4.10)

\displaystyle((1,\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(g^{-1}))\circ((\lambda,\mu),\mathrm{id})\circ((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(g))=((\lambda^{-1}\mu,\lambda^{-1}),\mathrm{id})=((g(\lambda),g(\mu)),\mathrm{id})\ \ \text{for}\ u=g,

(4.11)

\displaystyle((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(f))\circ((\lambda,\mu),\mathrm{id})\circ((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(f))=((\mu^{-1},\lambda^{-1}),\mathrm{id})=((f(\lambda),f(\mu)),\mathrm{id})\ \ \text{for}\ u=f,

which gives
\mu=f(\lambda^{-1})=\lambda g(\lambda)
and
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=1
, which is equivalent to
\lambda\in(\textbf{F}^{*})^{gf}
,
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=1
. In the second case, we respectively get

(4.12)

\displaystyle((1,\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(g^{-1}))\circ((\lambda,\mu),\epsilon(h))\circ((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(g))=((\lambda^{-1}\mu,\lambda^{-1}\xi^{-2}),\epsilon(h))=((g(\lambda),g(\mu)),\epsilon(h)),

(4.13)

\displaystyle((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(f))\circ((\lambda,\mu),\epsilon(h))\circ((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(f))=((\xi^{-2}\mu^{-1},\xi^{-2}\lambda^{-1}),\epsilon(h))=((f(\lambda),f(\mu)),\epsilon(h)),

which implies
\mu=\lambda g(\lambda),\,\xi^{-2}=\lambda g(\mu)=\Norm_{g}(\lambda)
.
Thus
\Norm_{g}(\xi\lambda)=\Norm_{g}(\xi)\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=\xi^{3}\cdot\xi^{-2}=\xi
, so
S
is
\mathbf{K}
-trivial. Therefore, if
S
is not
\mathbf{K}
-trivial then
\Phi(\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S))
consists only of toric automorphisms.

(3)

Once again, every automorphism in
\Phi(\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S))
is of the form
\gamma=((\lambda,\mu),\mathrm{id})
, or
\gamma=((\lambda,\mu),\epsilon(h))
. Suppose that our automorphism group contains a toric automorphism; then the conjugation by
\alpha_{f}
gives:

(4.14)

\displaystyle((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(f))\circ((\lambda,\mu),\mathrm{id})\circ((\xi^{-1},\xi^{-1}),\epsilon(f))=((\mu^{-1},\lambda^{-1}),\mathrm{id})=((f(\lambda),f(\mu)),\epsilon(h)),

together with (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) this gives that all the toric automorphisms are as in
T_{3}
similar to the toric case in
(2)
with the only difference being the additional assumption that the
h
-Norms are
1
. As in the previous case, we observe that if
\Phi(\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S))
contains an element of the form
((\lambda,\mu),\epsilon(h))
, then (
4.12
) implies that
\xi
is a
g
-norm. Hence, by Lemma
2.9

(3)
, we may put
\xi=1
without loss of generality.

Further, the conjugation by
\alpha_{f}
gives

(4.15)

\displaystyle((1,1),\epsilon(f))\circ((\lambda,\mu),\epsilon(h))\circ((1,1),\epsilon(f))=((\mu^{-1},\lambda^{-1}),\epsilon(h))=((f(\lambda),f(\mu)),\epsilon(h)),

which is equivalent to
\mu=f(\lambda^{-1})
. We claim that
\alpha_{h}
belongs to
\Phi(\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S))
. Indeed, the two conditions required by (
4.12
)
are satisfied, because
\xi=1
and hence
\Norm_{g}(\rho)=1
. Further, the second system in (
4.9
) is satisfied because
\lambda=\rho
is
h
-invariant (and
\mu=\lambda g(\lambda)=\rho g(\rho)
for
\alpha_{h}
). So, it remains to check that
\mu=f(\lambda^{-1})
, which is equivalent to
g(\rho)\Norm_{f}(\rho)=1
. But
g(\rho)\Norm_{f}(\rho)N_{h}(\xi)=1
, as was found in our parametrization of
S
and since
\xi=1
this implies that
\alpha_{h}
belongs to
\Phi(\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S))
. ∎

5.
Closed points

5.1.
Generalities

We start with recalling some general facts. The
index

\indexx(X)
of an algebraic variety
X
over
\mathbf{k}
is the greatest common divisor of the degrees of all closed points on
X
, or equivalently, the greatest common divisor of the degrees of all finite field extensions
\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k}
such that
X
has a point over
\mathbf{E}
. It is well known that index is a birational invariant
[
GLL13
, Proposition 6.8]
.

Remark 5.1
.

Let
S
be a del Pezzo surface of degree 6 over
\mathbf{k}
.

(1)

Recall that
|-K_{S}|
gives an embedding of
S
into
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{k}}^{6}
as a surface of degree 6. By intersecting this surface by two hyperplanes in general position, we see that
S
has a point of degree dividing
6
(alternatively, we can consider the Galois action on the vertices of
\Sigma
). In particular, the index of a sextic del Pezzo surface always divides 6. If
S
is a
G
-surface, then
S
actually has a point of degree 6, as we have a transitive action of
\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{k}}/\mathbf{k})
on the vertices of
\Sigma
.

(2)

If
X(\mathbf{k})\neq\varnothing
, then
\indexx(X)=1
. The converse is known to fail in general, but for a del Pezzo surface
S
of degree 6 one can argue as in
[
Cor77
]
.
Suppose that
\indexx(S)=1
. If
S
has a point of degree
d
, then the proof of
[
Cor77
, Théorème]
shows that
S
contains an elliptic curve with an effective divisor of degree
3\gcd(d,2)
. We can assume that there is a point of odd degree on
S
, as otherwise
\indexx(S)\neq 1
. Therefore
S
has an effective zero-cycle of degree 3 and hence
S(\mathbf{k})\neq\varnothing
as explained in
[
Cor77
, §2]
.

The following classical statement is known as the Lang-Nishimura lemma.

Lemma 5.2

(
[
Lan54
,
Nis55
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{k}
be a field, and let
\phi\colon X^{\prime}\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,X
be a rational map of

\mathbf{k}
-schemes. Assume that
X^{\prime}
has a smooth
\mathbf{k}
-point and that
X
is proper. Then
X(\mathbf{k})\neq\varnothing
.

Finally, there is an explicit criterion of
\mathbf{k}
-rationality for a minimal geometrically rational surfaces:

Theorem 5.3

(
[
Isk96
]
)
.

A minimal geometrically rational surface
S
over a perfect field
\mathbf{k}
is
\mathbf{k}
-rational if and only if the following two conditions are satisfied:

(i):

S(\mathbf{k})\neq\varnothing
;

(ii):

K_{S}^{2}\geqslant 5
.

Corollary 5.4
.

A sextic
G
-del Pezzo surface
S
is
\mathbf{k}
-rational if and only if
S(\mathbf{k})\neq\varnothing
.

Definition 5.5
.

Let
S
be a non-
\mathbf{k}
-rational
G
-del Pezzo surface of degree 6. Consider its Severi-Brauer data from Definition
3.4
. If the Severi-Brauer surface
X
is trivial, i.e.
X\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{K}}^{2}
, we say that
S
is
\mathbf{K}
-trivial
. If the involution surface
Y
is trivial, i.e.
Y\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{L}}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{L}}^{1}
, then we say that
S
is
\mathbf{L}
-trivial
. Note that these properties do not depend on the choice of
X
or
Y
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
S
be a
G
-del Pezzo surface of degree 6 over
\mathbf{k}
, and assume that
S
is not
\mathbf{k}
-rational
(
equivalently,
S(\mathbf{k})=\varnothing
)
. The following statements are equivalent.

(1)

S
is
\mathbf{K}
-trivial;

(2)

\indexx(S)=2
;

(3)

S
has a degree 2 point.

Proof.

First,
(1)
implies
(3)
, because
S
being
\mathbf{K}
-trivial implies, by the Lang-Nishimura lemma, that
S(\mathbf{K})\neq\varnothing
. Since
S(\mathbf{k})=\varnothing
, we get a quadratic point on
S
. Next,
(3)
implies
(2)
, because
\indexx(S)\neq 1
by Remark
5.1
. Finally,
(2)
implies
(1)
. Indeed, if
\indexx(S)=2
then
S
has a point whose degree is not divisible by 3. But then the same holds for
X
, hence
X\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{K}}^{2}
by Proposition
2.5
.
∎

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
S
be a
G
-del Pezzo surface of degree 6 over
\mathbf{k}
, and assume that
S
is not
\mathbf{k}
-rational. The following statements are equivalent.

(1)

S
is
\mathbf{L}
-trivial.

(2)

\indexx(S)=3
.

(3)

S
has a degree 3 point.

Proof.

First,
(1)
implies
(3)
. Indeed, suppose that
S_{\mathbf{L}}
is birational to
Y=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{L}}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{L}}^{1}
. Then
S(\mathbf{L})\neq\varnothing
by the Lang-Nishimura lemma and hence in the case
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
the
g
-orbit of an
\mathbf{L}
-rational point gives a point of degree 3 on
S
, as
S(\mathbf{k})=\varnothing
. Assume that
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
. In this case, we consider the birational contraction
S_{\mathbf{E}}\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,Z
over the field
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle h,f\rangle}\subset\mathbf{L}
, see Section
3.2
. Then
Z
is a del Pezzo surface of degree 8 with
\rk\Pic_{\mathbf{E}}(Z)=1
such that
Z_{\mathbf{L}}\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{L}}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{L}}^{1}
and by Proposition
2.10
one has
Z\simeq\Weil_{\mathbf{L}/\mathbf{E}}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{L}}^{1}
, see
[
SV18
, Lemma 7.3]
for details. Therefore,
Z(\mathbf{E})\neq\varnothing
and thus
S(\mathbf{E})\neq\varnothing
. Now the
g
-orbit of an
\mathbf{E}
-rational point gives a 3-point on
S
.
Next,
(3)
implies
(2)
, because
\indexx(S)\neq 1
by Remark
5.1
. Finally,
(2)
implies
(1)
. Indeed, if
\indexx(S)=3
then
S
has a point of odd degree, hence the same is true for
Y
. But then
Y(\mathbf{L})\neq\varnothing
by Springer’s theorem
[
Spr52
]
.
∎

Finally, we have the following property.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
S
be a
G
-del Pezzo surface of degree 6 over
\mathbf{k}
, and assume that
S
is not
\mathbf{k}
-rational. The following statements are equivalent.

(1)

S
is neither
\mathbf{K}
-trivial, nor
\mathbf{L}
-trivial.

(2)

\indexx(S)=6
.

(3)

S
is birationally super-rigid, i.e.
\Bir_{\mathbf{k}}(S)=\Aut_{\mathbf{k}}(S)
.

Proof.

The equivalence of
(1)
and
(2)
follows from Propositions
5.6
and
5.7
, and the fact that
\indexx(S)
divides 6. Their equivalence to
(3)
follows from the Sarkisov program for
S
.
∎

5.2.
Explicit parametrization

Now we want to look at
d
-points on
S
. For their image under
\varphi
we can use the coordinates
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
defined over some field that lies over
\mathbf{F}
. This point then corresponds to
([1:\lambda_{1}:\lambda_{2}],[1:\lambda_{1}^{-1}:\lambda_{2}^{-1}])
.

Notation 5.9
.

In what follows, we fix the twisted action
\varphi\colon S_{\mathbf{F}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}}M
as in (
2.2
). Further,
\mathbf{E}
will denote the splitting field of a degree
d
point on
S
. If
\mathbf{E}\nsubseteq\mathbf{F}
then the generators of
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
of order 3 and 2 will be denoted
w
and
t
, respectively, as in Remark
2.3
. We will consider the
\varphi
-image of a
d
-point
p
on
S
, whose geometric components are denoted
p_{1},\ldots,p_{d}
,
d\in\{2,3\}
. By a slight abuse of notation, we mean that
\varphi
is naturally extended to the composite field
\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E}
when
\mathbf{E}\nsubseteq\mathbf{F}
. We set
\varphi(p_{1})=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, where
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E}^{*}
. When
\mathbf{E}\nsubseteq\mathbf{F}
we will assume that
t
is chosen so that it fixes
p_{1}
.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
S
be a non-
\mathbf{k}
-rational sextic
\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
-del Pezzo surface.

(1)

If
S
is
\mathbf{K}
-trivial, then there is a
2
-point on
S
. If a 2-point splits over a subfield of
\mathbf{F}
, then this field must be
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{F}^{g}
and the
\varphi
-image of this point is given by the
\alpha_{h}\circ h
-orbit of the point
(\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda))
, where
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}^{*}
,
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=1
.

The
\varphi
-image of
2
-points over another quadratic splitting field
\mathbf{E}\nsubseteq\mathbf{F}
is given by the
t
-orbit of the point
(\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda))
, where
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E}^{*}
,
\ \Norm_{g}(\lambda)=1
,
\Norm_{h}(\lambda)=\rho
.

(2)

If
S
is
\mathbf{L}
-trivial, then there is a
3
-point on
S
. If a 3-point splits over a subfield of
\mathbf{F}
, then this field must be
\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{F}^{h}
and the
\varphi
-image of this point is given by the
\alpha_{g}\circ g
-orbit of
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, where
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbf{F}^{*}
,
\Norm_{h}(\lambda_{1})=\Norm_{h}(\lambda_{2})=1.

The
\varphi
-image of
3
-points with some other splitting field
\mathbf{E}\nsubseteq\mathbf{F}
(of degree
3
or
6
) is given by the
w
-orbit of the point
(\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda))
, where
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E}^{*}
,
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=\xi^{-1}
,
\Norm_{h}(\lambda)=1
. If
\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k}
is of degree
6
, then we have two more conditions. If
\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{k}
, then
t(\lambda)=\lambda
. If
\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{K}
, then the second and third components of
\varphi(p)
are permuted by
\alpha_{h}\circ h
.

(3)

If
S
is neither
\mathbf{K}
-trivial, nor
\mathbf{L}
-trivial, then
\indexx(S)=6
.

Proof.

If
p
is a 2-point (respectively, a 3-point) split over
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{F}^{g}
(respectively, over
\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{F}^{h}
), then
q=\varphi(p)
satisfies

q=\varphi\circ u\circ\varphi^{-1}(q)=\alpha_{u}u(q),

where
u=g
for 2-points and
u=h
for 3-points. So, we start by looking at pairs
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
which are fixed by either
\alpha_{g}\circ g
, or
\alpha_{h}\circ h
. We have

(5.1)

(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\alpha_{g}(g(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}))=\left(\frac{1}{\xi g(\lambda_{2})},\frac{g(\lambda_{1})}{\xi g(\lambda_{2})}\right)

if and only if
\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{1}g(\lambda_{1})
and
\Norm_{g}(\lambda_{1}^{-1})=\xi
. We have

(5.2)

(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\alpha_{h}(h(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}))=\left(\frac{\rho}{h(\lambda_{1})},\frac{\rho g(\rho)}{h(\lambda_{2})}\right)

if and only if
\Norm_{h}(\lambda_{1})=\rho
,
\Norm_{h}(\lambda_{2})=\rho g(\rho)
. We now consider 3 cases from the statement.

(1)

A
\mathbf{K}
-rational point on the Severi-Brauer surface gives rise to a
2
-point on
S
. Let
p=\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
be a
2
-point on
S
that splits over
\mathbf{K}
and put
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\varphi(p_{1})
. Since
p_{1}
is fixed by
g
, the condition (
5.1
) holds, hence we get, using
\xi=1
, that
\lambda_{1}=\lambda\in\mathbf{F}^{*},

\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=1
,
\lambda_{2}=\lambda g(\lambda)
. On the other hand,
p_{2}=h(p_{1})
and thus (
5.2
) implies
\varphi(p_{2})=\alpha_{h}(h(p_{1}))=(\rho h(\lambda^{-1}),\rho h(\lambda^{-1})g(\rho h(\lambda^{-1})))
.

Now let
p
be a
2
-point that does not split over
\mathbf{K}
. Then its splitting field
\mathbf{E}
is quadratic extension of
\mathbf{k}
and
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2
. So,
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\varphi(p_{1})
is invariant under
\alpha_{g}\circ g
and
\alpha_{h}\circ h
, so
\Norm_{g}(\lambda_{1})=1
,
\Norm_{h}(\lambda_{1})=\rho
, and
\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{1}g(\lambda_{1})
, where
\lambda_{1}\in\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E}
. Note that
p_{2}=t(p_{1})
, but since
t
acts trivially on
\mathbf{F}
and
\varphi
is defined over
\mathbf{F}
, we have that the action of
t
is not twisted by
\varphi
.

(2)

An
\mathbf{L}
-rational point on an involution surface gives rise to a
3
-point on
S
. Let
p=\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\}
be a
3
-point, and as before
p_{1}=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
. Since
p_{1}
is fixed by
\alpha_{h}\circ h
, we can use the condition (
5.2
) and
\rho=1
to deduce that
\Norm_{h}(\lambda_{1})=\Norm_{h}(\lambda_{2})=1
. The other two components
p_{2}
and
p_{3}
are represented by the image of
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
under
\alpha_{g}\circ g
and
\alpha_{g^{2}}\circ g^{2}
which is exactly what is stated in the Proposition.

Now let
p
be a 3-point that does not split over
\mathbf{L}
, and let
\mathbf{E}
be its splitting field. Then
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
or
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\Sym_{3}
. In the first case, we have either
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{E}
or
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{k}
, but the former case forces
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{L}
, which was already discussed above. So, we assume
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{k}
and let
w
be a generator of
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
. As before, letting
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\varphi(p_{1})
, we notice that
g
and
h
act trivially on
p
, so (
5.1
) gives
\lambda_{1}=\lambda
,
\lambda_{2}=\lambda g(\lambda)
and
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=\xi^{-1}
, while (
5.2
) gives
\Norm_{h}(\lambda)=1
(recall that
\rho=1
). Taking a
w
-orbit of
p_{1}
, we get the 3-point from the statement.

In the second case
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\Sym_{3}
, let
w
be a generator of order 3 and
t
be a generator of order 2. If
\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{k}
then we argue as in the previous paragraph to get that a 3-point is of the said form (note that
g
and
h
act trivially on
p
), and choose
t
so that it fixes
p_{1}
, and permutes
p_{2}
and
p_{3}
. Suppose that
\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{F}^{g}
. As was mentioned in Remark
2.3
, the group
\Gal(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
, viewed as a subgroup of
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\times\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
, is generated by
(g,\mathrm{id})
,
(\mathrm{id},w)
and
(h,t)
. So, let
\varphi(p_{1})=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, where
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E}^{*}
. Note that
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
is
(g,\mathrm{id})
-invariant, which gives
\lambda_{1}=\lambda
,
\lambda_{2}=\lambda g(\lambda)
and
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=\xi^{-1}
as before. As we choose
t
to preserve
p_{1}
and permute
p_{2}
with
p_{3}
, we find that
(\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda))
is
\alpha_{h}\circ h
-invariant, hence
\Norm_{h}(\lambda)=1
. By contrast,
p_{2}
is sent onto
p_{3}
by
(h,t)
, hence the claim.

(3)
Follows from Proposition
5.8
.
∎

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
S
be a non-
\mathbf{k}
-rational sextic
\Sym_{3}
-del Pezzo surface. Then there is a
3
-point on
S
. If such point splits over a subfield of
\mathbf{F}
, then it must be
\mathbf{F}
itself. The
\varphi
-image of such 3-point is given by the
\alpha_{g}\circ g
-orbit of the point
\left(\lambda,f(\lambda^{-1})\xi^{-1}\right)
, where
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}^{*}
.

The
\varphi
-image of 3-points with some other splitting field
\mathbf{E}\nsubseteq\mathbf{F}

(
of degree
3
or
6
)
is given by the
w
-orbit of the point
(\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda))
, where
w
is an order
3
element in
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
,
\lambda\in(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E}^{*})^{gf}
,
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=\xi^{-1}
.

If
\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k}
is of degree
6

(
thus the Galois group is
\Sym_{3}
)
, then we have two more conditions. If
\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{k}
, then
t(\lambda)=\lambda
. If
\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{K}
, then the second and third components are permuted by
\alpha_{f}\circ f
.

Proof.

Any three vertices at distance 2 from each other in the graph
\Sigma
give a
3
-point on
S
, hence
\indexx(S)=3
by Proposition
5.7
. Let
\mathbf{E}
be the splitting field of a 3-point on
S
. Then one of the following holds:

a)

\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}
, hence
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}
.

b)

\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{k}
.

c)

\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}
is quadratic over
\mathbf{k}
, i.e.
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}^{g}=\mathbf{K}
. Then
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\Sym_{3}
.

d)

\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}
is cubic over
\mathbf{k}
. This case is not possible since
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}
must be Galois over
\mathbf{k}
.

Suppose that we are in case (a). A 3-point that splits over
\mathbf{F}
has one component fixed by
f
. Thus we solve
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\alpha_{f}\circ f(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
and we get

(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\alpha_{f}\circ f(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\left(\frac{1}{\xi f(\lambda_{2})},\frac{1}{\xi f(\lambda_{1})}\right)

which is equivalent to
\lambda_{2}=\xi^{-1}f(\lambda_{1}^{-1})
; we set
\lambda_{1}=\lambda
. Calculating further the
\alpha_{g}\circ g
-orbit of this pair, we get the points from the statement.

Now assume that there is a
3
-point that splits over a field
\mathbf{E}\neq\mathbf{F}
, i.e. we are in one of the cases (b) or (c), so either
\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{k}
or
\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}^{g}=\mathbf{K}
and
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\Sym_{3}
. As before, we let
w
be an order 3 generator in
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
and
t
be an order 2 generator, if exists. If
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{k}
then we argue as in
\mathbb{Z}/6
case, taking
t\in\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
a transposition that fixes
p_{1}
. Suppose that
\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{K}
. By Remark
2.3
, the group
\Gal(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
, viewed as a subgroup of
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\times\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
, is generated by
(g,\mathrm{id})
,
(\mathrm{id},w)
and
(f,t)
. The proof finishes as in Proposition
5.10
by noticing that

\left(\lambda,\frac{1}{f(\lambda)\xi}\right)=\alpha_{g}\circ g\left(\lambda,\frac{1}{f(\lambda)\xi}\right)=(gf(\lambda),g(\lambda)gf(\lambda)),

is equivalent to
gf(\lambda)=\lambda
,
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=\xi^{-1}
.
∎

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
S
be a non-
\mathbf{k}
-rational sextic
\mathrm{D}_{6}
-del Pezzo surface. Then one of the following holds.

(1)

If
S
is
\mathbf{K}
-trivial then there is a
2
-point on
S
. If a 2-point splits over a subfield of
\mathbf{F}
, then this field is either
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,s\rangle}
, or
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,f\rangle}
, i.e. there are no
2
-points that split over
\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,h\rangle}
. The
\varphi
-image of all
2
-points split over
\mathbf{K}
is given by the
\alpha_{h}\circ h
-orbit of the point
(\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda))
, where
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=1
,
\Norm_{gs}(\lambda)=\rho
. The
\varphi
-image of all
2
-points split over
\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,f\rangle}
is given by the
\alpha_{h}\circ h
-orbit of the point
(\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda))
, where
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=1
,
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}^{gf}
.

The
\varphi
-image of 2-points with some other quadratic splitting field
\mathbf{E}\nsubseteq\mathbf{F}
is given by the
t
-orbit of
(\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda))
, where
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E}^{*}
,
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=1
,
\Norm_{h}(\lambda)=\rho
,
\Norm_{gs}(\lambda)=\rho
.

(2)

If
S
is
\mathbf{L}
-trivial, then there is a
3
-point on
S
. If a 3-point splits over a subfield of
\mathbf{F}
, then this field must be
\mathbf{F}^{h}
. The
\varphi
-image of this point is given by the
\alpha_{g}\circ g
-orbit of
(\lambda,f(\lambda^{-1})\xi^{-1})
, where
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}^{*}
and
\Norm_{h}(\lambda)=1
.

If a 3-point splits over some other field
\mathbf{E}\nsubseteq\mathbf{F}
, then its
\varphi
-image is given by the
w
-orbit of
(\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda))
, where
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E}^{*}
,
\rho=\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=\Norm_{h}(\lambda)=\Norm_{gs}(\lambda)=1
. If
\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k}
is of degree 6, then we have two more conditions. If
\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{k}
then
t(\lambda)=\lambda
. If
\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{E}
is of degree
2
over
\mathbf{k}
with the Galois group generated by
v\in\{f,s,h\}
then the second and third components are permuted by
\alpha_{v}\circ v
.

(3)

If
S
is neither
\mathbf{K}
-trivial, nor
\mathbf{L}
-trivial, then
\indexx(S)=6
.

Proof.

(1)
We assume that
\xi=1
. If
\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k}
is a splitting field of a point of degree 2, then either
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{k}
, or
\mathbf{E}\subset\mathbf{F}
and in this case
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,u\rangle}
, where
u\in\{h,f,s\}
. Suppose that we are in the latter case, let
p=\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
be a 2-point split over
\mathbf{E}
and let
\varphi(p_{1})=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
. Then, as before, we notice that
p_{1}
is
\alpha_{g}\circ g
-invariant, which gives the conditions
\lambda_{1}=\lambda
,
\lambda_{2}=\lambda g(\lambda)
,
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=1
. Suppose that
u=f
. Then
\alpha_{f}\circ f
-invariance translates to

\lambda g(\lambda)f(\lambda)=1

which implies (since
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=1
) that
f(\lambda)=g^{2}(\lambda)
, hence
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}^{gf}
. If
u=s
then, using parametrization of Proposition
3.17
with
\xi=1
, we get

\alpha_{s}=\alpha_{h}h(\alpha_{f})=((\rho,\rho g(\rho)),\epsilon(hf)).

Therefore, the
\alpha_{s}\circ s
-invariance of
(\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda))
means

(\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda))=(\rho s(\lambda)sg(\lambda),\rho g(\rho)s(\lambda)),

hence
\lambda=\rho s(\lambda)sg(\lambda)
. By using
\Norm_{g}(s(\lambda))=1
, we notice that two equalities
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=1
and
\lambda=\rho s(\lambda)sg(\lambda)
are equivalent to
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=1
and
\Norm_{gs}(\lambda)=\rho
.

Finally, the
\alpha_{h}\circ h
-invariance of
(\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda))
implies

(\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda))=(\rho h(\lambda^{-1}),\rho g(\rho)h(\lambda^{-1})hg(\lambda^{-1})),

which is equivalent to
\rho=\Norm_{h}(\lambda)
. But this implies that
S
is
\mathbf{L}
-trivial, hence
\mathbf{k}
-rational, a contradiction.

Now, in the former case
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{k}
, we again let
\varphi(p_{1})=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
and notice that this point is
\alpha_{u}\circ u
-invariant for
u\in\{g,h,f\}
, and is sent to
\varphi(p_{2})
by
t
.

(2)
: We assume that
\rho=1
. Let
\mathbf{E}
be the splitting field of a 3-point on
S
. Since
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}
is Galois over
\mathbf{k}
, one of the following holds:

a)

\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}
, hence
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}
and in this case we necessarily have
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}^{h}
.

b)

\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{k}
.

c)

\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}
is quadratic over
\mathbf{k}
, i.e.
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,u\rangle}=\mathbf{K}
where
u\in\{h,f,s\}
.

In the case (a), we notice that
\varphi(p_{1})=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
is
\alpha_{h}\circ h
-invariant, hence we get the conditions
\Norm_{h}(\lambda_{1})=\Norm_{h}(\lambda_{2})=1
. Assuming that
\alpha_{f}\circ f
fixes
p_{1}
and swaps
p_{2}
with
p_{3}
, we get
\lambda_{1}f(\lambda_{2})\xi=1
. Now the
\alpha_{g}\circ g
-orbit of
(\lambda_{1},\lambda)
gives a 3-point on
S
.

In the case (b), we notice that
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
is
\alpha_{u}\circ u
-invariant for
u\in\{g,h,f\}
, hence we get conditions on
\lambda
from the statement. The 3-point is then generated by the
w
-orbit of
(\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda))
, and we have an extra condition
t(\lambda)=\lambda
. Finally, in the case (c) where
\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{E}
is quadratic over
\mathbf{k}
, Remark
2.3
gives the generators of
\Gal(\mathbf{E}\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})
. Notice that
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
is both
(g,\mathrm{id})
- and
(u,\mathrm{id})
-invariant, while
(f,t)
(for
u=h
) or
(h,t)
(for
u=s
and
u=f
) preserves
p_{1}
and swaps
p_{2}
with
p_{3}
. Hence the result.
∎

5.3.
Closed points in general position

Given
n\leqslant 6
distinct points
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}
, we
will say that they are in general position if no three of them are contained in a line, and for
n=6

all six are not contained in a conic.
If
p\in\mathbb{P}_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}^{2}
is a point of degree
d
in general position, then its blow-up is a del Pezzo surface of degree
9-d
, see for example
[
Man86
, Theorem IV.2.6]
. Given a sextic
G
-del Pezzo surface
S
, we will say that a point
p\in S
of degree
d
is
in general position
on
S
if its blow-up is a del Pezzo surface (whose
G
-invariant Picard rank is then 2). An interesting feature of degree 3 and degree 6 points on non-trivial Severi-Brauer surfaces is that they are automatically in general position and hence can serve as a centre of a Sarkisov link
[
Shr20
, Lemma 2.8]
. Let us investigate the position of points on sextic del Pezzo surfaces.

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
S
be a non-
\mathbf{k}
-rational sextic
G
-del Pezzo surface. Then one has the following.

(1)

Suppose that
\indexx(S)=2
. Then any 2-point on
S
is in general position.

(2)

Suppose that
\indexx(S)=3
. If
p\in S
is a 3-point that does not split over
\mathbf{F}
, then
p
is in general position. Otherwise, we have the following subcases (in the notation of Propositions
5.10
,
5.11
and
5.12
, and the restrictions on
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}
, given therein).

(
i
)

If
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
, then
p
