is in general position if and only if

\lambda_{2}\neq\lambda_{1}g(\lambda_{1}),\,\lambda_{1}\neq\xi\lambda_{2}g^{2}(\lambda_{2}),\ \xi g(\lambda_{1})g^{2}(\lambda_{2})\neq 1.

(
ii
)

If
G\simeq\Sym_{3}
or
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
, then
p
is in general position if and only if

\xi\lambda g(\lambda)f(\lambda)\neq 1,\ \lambda\not\in\mathbf{F}^{gf}.

Proof.

(1)
If
p
splits over
\mathbf{F}
, then
\varphi(p)
is the
\alpha_{h}\circ h
orbit of
(\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda))
,
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}^{*}
with different conditions on
\lambda
depending on the group
G
, see Propositions
5.10
and
5.12
. By viewing
S_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}
as the blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}
, we should study the position of the points

\left[1:\lambda:\lambda g(\lambda))\right],\ \left[1:\frac{\rho}{h(\lambda)}:\frac{\rho}{h(\lambda)}g\left(\frac{\rho}{h(\lambda)}\right)\right],\ [1:0:0],\ [0:1:0],\ [0:0:1],

namely we need to check whether any three of them are colinear. The only non-obvious triple to check is the first two points and a coordinate point, which (by applying
\alpha_{g}\circ g
, if needed) we can assume to be
[1:0:0]
. In that case, the
x_{1}
- and
x_{2}
-components of the first two points must satisfy the equation
x_{1}+ax_{2}=0
for some
a\in\mathbf{k}^{*}
, which implies
\rho=\Norm_{h}(\lambda)
and hence
S
is
\mathbf{L}
-trivial, a contradiction.

If
p
splits over a different field
\mathbf{E}
, then we have that
\varphi(p)
is the
t
orbit of
(\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda)),\,\lambda\in(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E})^{*}
with different conditions on
\lambda
depending on the case of
G
. As in the previous case, we need to examine the position of points

[1:\lambda:\lambda g(\lambda))],\ [1:t(\lambda):t(\lambda)g(t(\lambda)))],\ [1:0:0],\ [0:1:0],\ [0:0:1]

on
\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}
. As before, we only check that the first three points are not collinear. If they are, then
t(\lambda)=\lambda
, which contradicts to
(\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda))
having a
t
-orbit of size 2.

(2)
First, we show that a 3-point
p\in S
cannot lie on a 1-curve
C\subset S
, the preimage of a conic on
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{F}}^{2}
. Suppose that
p\in C
and consider the blow-down
\pi\colon S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,X
over
\mathbf{K}
, where
T
is a non-trivial Severi-Brauer surface. Note that
C
passes through the three
(-1)
-curves on
S
which are blow-down, and
\pi(p)
is a 3-point on
X
. Hence
C
contains two
3
-points over
\mathbf{K}
, and therefore
C
is
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{K})
-invariant. But this is impossible (for example, because a Sarkisov 3-link at any of these 3-points maps
C
to a
\mathbf{K}
-line on
X^{\rm op}
), hence
X
must be trivial. Suppose that
p
splits over
\mathbf{E}\not\subset\mathbf{F}
. Then
\varphi(p)
is the
w
-orbit of
(\lambda,\lambda g(\lambda))
,
\lambda\in(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E})^{*}
with different conditions on
\lambda
depending on
G
. We check that there are no collinear triples among the points

[1:\lambda:\lambda g(\lambda))],\ [1:w(\lambda):w(\lambda)wg(\lambda))],\ [1:w^{2}(\lambda):w^{2}(\lambda)w^{2}g(\lambda))],\ [1:0:0],\ [0:1:0],\ [0:0:1]

As above, we check this for the first two points and
[1:0:0]
. If these points were collinear, then we would have
w(\lambda)=\lambda
which again gives a contradiction.

Finally, we consider
3
-points which split over
\mathbf{F}
. In the case
(2)(i)
, the components of
\varphi(p)
are

(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\ \left(\frac{1}{g(\lambda_{2}\xi)},g\left(\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\lambda_{2}\xi}\right)\right),\,\left(g^{2}\left(\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}}\right),\frac{1}{g^{2}(\lambda_{1}\xi)}\right).

If
x_{1}+Ax_{2}=0
is a line through
[1:0:0]
and the first two components above, then we mus have
A=-\lambda_{1}/\lambda_{2}
and
\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{1}g(\lambda_{1})
. Similarly, we find that if
[1:0:0]
is collinear with the second and the third components, then
A=-1/g(\lambda_{1})
and
\xi g(\lambda_{1})g^{2}(\lambda_{2})=1
. If
[1:0:0]
is collinear with the first and the third components, then
\xi\lambda_{2}g^{2}(\lambda_{2})=\lambda_{1}
.

In the cases
(2)(ii)
, the components of
\varphi(p)
are

\left(\lambda,\frac{1}{f(\lambda)\xi}\right),\big(gf(\lambda),g(\lambda f(\lambda))\big),\left(\frac{1}{g^{2}(\lambda f(\lambda))\xi},\frac{1}{\xi g^{2}(\lambda)}\right),

where
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}^{*}
and
\Norm_{h}(\lambda)=1
when
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
. As in the previous case, we check when the components of any two of these points simultaneously satisfy the equation
x_{1}+Ax_{2}=0
. The result follows.
∎

Proposition 5.14
.

Let
S
be a sextic
G
-del Pezzo surface of index 3. Then it has
3
-points in general position whose splitting field is a subfield of
\mathbf{F}
, namely
\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{F}^{h}
when
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
, or
\mathbf{F}
itself when
G\simeq\Sym_{3}
, or
\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{F}^{h}
when
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
.

Proof.

We consider different possibilities for
G
, following the notation of Proposition
5.13
.

\bullet

If
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
, then we look for
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbf{F}^{*}
such that
\Norm_{h}(\lambda_{1})=\Norm_{h}(\lambda_{2})=1
and the condition of
(2)(i)
is satisfied. Choose
\lambda_{1}=b/h(b)
, where
b\in(\mathbf{F}^{g})^{*}
, and
\lambda_{2}=\xi^{-1}
(recall that we may assume
\rho=1
in (
3.9
) for index 3 surfaces). We therefore get the inequalities:

\xi^{-1}\neq\frac{b^{2}}{h(b^{2})},\ \frac{b}{h(b)}\neq\xi^{-1},\ \frac{b}{h(b)}\neq 1.

To satisfy the third one, it is enough to choose
b\notin L_{1}=\mathbf{k}^{*}
. Since
\Norm_{h}(\xi^{-1})=1
and
\xi\in\mathbf{F}^{g}
, Theorem
2.4
implies that there exists
a\in(\mathbf{F}^{g})^{*}
such that
a/h(a)=\xi^{-1}
. Note that for any other
a^{\prime}\in\mathbf{F}^{g}
such that
a^{\prime}/h(a^{\prime})=\xi^{-1}
one has
a/a^{\prime}\in\mathbf{F}^{g,h}=\mathbf{k}
, so all elements
b\in\mathbf{F}^{g}
satisfying the second condition lie in
1
-dimensional subspace
L_{2}\subset\mathbf{F}^{g}
.

If
\xi^{-1}
is not a square in
\mathbf{F}^{g}
, then there are no
b
satisfying the equality
\xi^{-1}=b^{2}/h(b^{2})
and we set
L_{3}=\varnothing
. Suppose that
\xi^{-1}=c^{2}
for some
c\in\mathbf{F}^{g}
. Then for all
b\in\mathbf{F}^{g}
satisfying
c^{2}=b^{2}/h(b^{2})
one has
b/h(b)=\pm c
. As above, the equality
b_{1}/h(b_{1})=c=b_{2}/h(b_{2})
implies
b_{1}/b_{2}\in\mathbf{F}^{g,h}=\mathbf{k}
and we conclude that the set
L_{3}
of all
b
satisfying the condition
\xi^{-1}=b^{2}/h(b^{2})
is of dimension at most 1. To sum up, since
\mathbf{F}^{g}
is of dimension
2
over
\mathbf{k}
, the set
\mathbf{F}^{g}\setminus\{L_{1}\cup L_{2}\cup L_{3}\}
is not empty, hence the existence of
b
and
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}
satisfying the desired restrictions.

\bullet

If
G\simeq\Sym_{3}
, then we take
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}^{*}\setminus\mathbf{F}^{gf}
and we only need to check that
\xi^{-1}\neq\lambda g(\lambda)f(\lambda)
. But if one has
\xi^{-1}=\lambda g(\lambda)f(\lambda)
, then the
f
-invariance of
\xi
implies that
\lambda f(\lambda)fg(\lambda)=f(\lambda f(\lambda)g(\lambda))=\lambda f(\lambda)g(\lambda)
which is equivalent to
gf(\lambda)=\lambda
, a contradiction.

\bullet

If
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
, then we look for the same
\lambda
as in the
\Sym_{3}
-case, with an additional property
\Norm_{h}(\lambda)=1
. Take
a\in\mathbf{F}
and put
\lambda=a/h(a)
. The property
\lambda\neq gf(\lambda)
is equivalent to
\Norm_{gs}(a)\notin\mathbf{F}^{h}
, so if the latter holds for
a
then we are done. Suppose that
\Norm_{gs}(a)\in\mathbf{F}^{h}
. Then take
b=a+1
. If
\Norm_{gs}(b)\notin\mathbf{F}^{h}
, then we choose
\lambda=b/h(b)
. Otherwise, we notice that

\Norm_{gs}(b)=\Norm_{gs}(a)+1+(a+gs(a))=h(\Norm_{gs}(b))=\Norm_{gs}(a)+1+h(a+gs(a)),

and therefore

a+\frac{N_{gs}(a)}{a}=h(a)+\frac{N_{gs}(a)}{h(a)}

which gives

\Norm_{h}(a)(h(a)-a)=\Norm_{gs}(a)(h(a)-a).

by multiplying with
N_{h}(a)
and rearranging.

Thus, either
a\in\mathbf{F}^{h}
or
\Norm_{h}(a)=\Norm_{gs}(a)
. The latter is equivalent to
gf(a)=a
, hence it is enough to choose
a\in\mathbf{F}\setminus(\mathbf{F}^{h}\cup\mathbf{F}^{gf})\neq\varnothing
.

∎

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
S
be a sextic
G
-del Pezzo surface of index 2. Then it has
2
-points in general position whose splitting field is a quadratic subfield of
\mathbf{F}
, namely
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{F}^{g}
when
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
, or
\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,f\rangle}
and
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,s\rangle}
when
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
.

Proof.

We again consider different possibilities for
G
, and use the notation of Proposition
5.10
. If
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
, then we can choose
\lambda=1
. The case
G\simeq\Sym_{3}
is not possible. Suppose that
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
.

Recall that we have two options for the splitting field
\mathbf{E}
of such a
2
-point. If we want
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,f\rangle}
, then we can take
\lambda=1
.
If we want
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{K}
, then we need
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}
such that
\Norm_{g}(\lambda)=1
,
\Norm_{gs}(\lambda)=\rho
. Let
\lambda=g(\rho^{-1})
. Since, by Proposition
3.17
, one has
\Norm_{g}(\rho)=1
and
g(\rho)\Norm_{f}(\rho)=1
, these properties are indeed satisfied.
∎

We will see in the last Section, that these two propositions guarantee the existence of self-links; this will be used in our construction of homomorphisms.

5.4.
A surface with many splitting fields of 3-points

In the following example, we construct, by using the explicit cocycle parametrization of sextic del Pezzo surfaces from above, an
\Sym_{3}
-surface that contains an uncountable number of 3-points in general position with pairwise distinct splitting fields. This example will later demonstrate that the surfaces mentioned in Theorem
C
do exist.

Example 5.16
.

Let
\mathbf{F}=\mathbb{C}(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},s)
and
G\subset\Aut(\mathbf{F})
be the subgroup which acts as
\Sym_{3}
on the variables
\{t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\}
and fixes
s
. We let
g=(123)
and
f=(23)
be the generators of
G
. Set
\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{F}^{G}
.

\bullet

First, we claim that
\xi=s
defines a non-
\mathbf{k}
-rational sextic
\Sym_{3}
-del Pezzo surface
S
over
\mathbf{k}
.

Indeed,
s\in\mathbf{k}^{*}
, hence by Propositions
3.13
it is enough to prove that
s\notin\Norm_{g}(\mathbf{F}^{*})
to show that
S
is not rational over
\mathbf{k}
. Assume that there are
P,Q\in\mathbb{C}[t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},s]
such that

s=\Norm_{g}\left(\frac{P}{Q}\right)=\frac{P(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},s)P(t_{2},t_{3},t_{1},s)P(t_{3},t_{1},t_{2},s)}{Q(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},s)Q(t_{2},t_{3},t_{1},s)Q(t_{3},t_{1},t_{2},s)}.

Multiplying both sides of the equation by the denominator of the fraction on the right, and considering the resulting expressions on both sides as polynomials in
s
with coefficients in
\mathbb{C}[t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}]
, we see that the highest degree of
s
on the right is divisible by 3, whereas on the left it is not. This is a contradiction.

\bullet

Next, for every
z\in\mathbb{C}
, define

\mathbf{E}_{z}=\mathbf{k}\big(\sqrt[3]{s(t_{1}+z)(t_{2}+z)(t_{3}+z)}\big),\ \ \lambda_{z}=\frac{\sqrt[3]{s(t_{1}+z)(t_{2}+z)(t_{3}+z)}}{t_{3}+z}

We claim that
\mathbf{E}_{z}
is a cubic Galois extension of
\mathbf{k}
and
\lambda_{z}^{-1}
yields a
3
-point on
S
in general position.

Since
s(t_{1}+z)(t_{2}+z)(t_{3}+z)
is
G
-invariant, it belongs to
\mathbf{k}^{*}
. Since
\mathbf{k}^{*}
contains a third root of unity, we immediately see that
\mathbf{E}_{z}
is of degree
3
over
\mathbf{k}
and is Galois. To prove that
\lambda_{z}^{-1}
yields a
3
-point we need, according to Proposition
5.11
, to prove that

\lambda_{z}\in(\mathbf{E}_{z}\mathbf{F})^{gf},\ \ \Norm_{g}(\lambda_{z})=s.

The invariance of
\lambda_{z}
under
gf=(12)
is obvious. We further compute

\Norm_{g}(\lambda_{z})=\frac{\sqrt[3]{s(t_{1}+z)(t_{2}+z)(t_{3}+z)}^{3}}{\Norm_{g}(t_{3}+z)}=\frac{s(t_{1}+z)(t_{2}+z)(t_{3}+z)}{(t_{1}+z)(t_{2}+z)(t_{3}+z)}=s,

as required. Notice that the resulting 3-point is in general position on
S
by Proposition
5.13
, since
\mathbf{E}_{z}
is not a subfield of
\mathbf{F}
.

\bullet

Finally, we claim that for
v\neq z
one has
\mathbf{E}_{z}\neq\mathbf{E}_{v}
inside of a fixed algebraic closure of
\mathbf{k}
.

Now we want to prove that for
v\neq z\in\mathbb{C}
we have that
\mathbf{E}_{z}\neq\mathbf{E}_{v}
. To do this it is enough to prove that
\sqrt[3]{s(t_{1}+z)(t_{2}+z)(t_{3}+z)}\not\in\mathbf{E}_{v}
.
Assume the converse holds, then there are
a,b,c\in\mathbf{k}^{*}
such that:

(5.3)

(t_{1}+z)(t_{2}+z)(t_{3}+z)=\left(a+b\sqrt[3]{s(t_{1}+v)(t_{2}+v)(t_{3}+v)}+c\sqrt[3]{s^{2}(t_{1}+v)^{2}(t_{2}+v)^{2}(t_{3}+v)^{2}}\right)^{3}.

Setting
\mu_{v}=s(t_{1}+v)(t_{2}+v)(t_{3}+v)
, the right hand side becomes

(a^{3}+b^{3}\mu_{v}+c^{3}\mu_{v}^{2}+6abc\mu_{v})+(3a^{2}b+3c^{2}a\mu_{v}+3b^{2}c\mu_{v})\sqrt[3]{\mu_{v}}+(3ab^{2}+3a^{2}c+3bc^{2}\mu_{v})\sqrt[3]{\mu_{v}^{2}}.

Now on the left hand side of (
5.3
) we have an element of the field
\mathbf{k}
, while on the right hand side a polynomial from
\mathbf{k}[\sqrt[3]{\mu_{v}}]
. This gives us the three equations

(5.4)

\displaystyle a^{3}+b^{3}\mu_{v}+c^{3}\mu_{v}^{2}+6abc\mu_{v}=(t_{1}+z)(t_{2}+z)(t_{3}+z)

(5.5)

\displaystyle 3a^{2}b+3c^{2}a\mu_{v}+3b^{2}c\mu_{v}=0

(5.6)

\displaystyle 3ab^{2}+3a^{2}c+3bc^{2}\mu_{v}=0

Let us prove that
a,b,c\neq 0
. If
a=0
then (
5.6
) implies
bc=0
, hence (
5.4
) gives either at
b^{3}\mu_{v}=\mu_{z}
or
c^{3}\mu_{v}^{2}=\mu_{z}
. The latter is impossible by comparing the degrees. The former is impossible since different irreducible components of
\mu_{z}
and
\mu_{v}
are not associates in the polynomial ring. By multiplying (
5.5
) by
b
and (
5.6
) by
a
, we get

3a^{2}b^{2}+3abc^{2}\mu_{v}+3b^{3}c\mu_{v}=0=3a^{2}b^{2}+3a^{3}c+3abc^{2}\mu_{v}

which simplifies to

b^{3}s(t_{1}+v)(t_{2}+v)(t_{3}+v)=a^{3},

which gives a contradiction by considering the degree of
s
of both sides modulo 3.

6.
Sarkisov links and birational classification

Let
S
be a del Pezzo surface of degree 6 over
\mathbf{k}
with
\Pic(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
, and
\phi\colon S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S^{\prime}
be a birational map to another Mori fibre space
S^{\prime}
. By
[
Isk96
, Theorem 2.6]
, the map
\phi
is a composition of automorphisms and the Sarkisov links of type II, and each of them is represented by the following diagram:

(6.1)

\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\prime}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm pt}}

where
\eta,\eta^{\prime}
are birational morphisms, namely the blow-ups of closed points of degrees
d
and
d^{\prime}
, and
S^{\prime},T
are del Pezzo surfaces as well. Moreover, one of the following holds:

(1)

S\simeq S^{\prime}
,
d=5
,
\chi
is a birational Bertini involution;

(2)

S\simeq S^{\prime}
,
d=4
,
\chi
is a birational Geiser involution;

(3)

d=3
,
K_{S^{\prime}}^{2}=6
;

(4)

d=2
,
K_{S^{\prime}}^{2}=6
;

(5)

d=1
,
S^{\prime}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
S
be a sextic del Pezzo surface with
\Pic(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. Suppose that
S
is not rational over
\mathbf{k}
, and let
\phi\colon S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S^{\prime}
be a map to another Mori fibre space
S^{\prime}
. Then
S^{\prime}
is a sextic del Pezzo surface with
\Pic(S^{\prime})\simeq\mathbb{Z}
, and one has the following:

(1)

If
\indexx(S)=6
, then
\phi
is an isomorphism.

(2)

If
\indexx(S)=2
, then
\phi
is a composition of isomorphisms and Sarkisov links of type II at points of degrees 2 or 4 (Geiser birational involutions).

(3)

If
\indexx(S)=3
, then
\phi
is a composition of isomorphisms and Sarkisov links of type II at points of degree 3.

Proof.

Follows from the classification of links and Propositions
5.6
,
5.7
, and
5.8
.
∎

Since Geiser birational involutions always lead to an isomorphic surface, our next goal is to understand how the Sarkisov links at points of degrees 2 and 3 change the Severi-Brauer data of del Pezzo surfaces. If
\mathbf{E}
is a splitting field of the centre
p
of our Sarkisov link (
2.3
), then all
(-1)
-curves on
T
are clearly defined over the composite field
\mathbf{E}\mathbf{F}
. However, the splitting field
\mathbf{F}^{\prime}
of the new hexagon
\Sigma^{\prime}\subset S^{\prime}
may be a proper subfield of
\mathbf{E}\mathbf{F}
.

Remark 6.2
.

In particular, as we will see below, a Sarkisov link can change the Galois action on the hexagon of
(-1)
-curves. In geometric setting, i.e. for complex
G
-minimal sextic del Pezzo surfaces acted on by a finite group
G\subset\Aut(S)
, this phenomena was already noticed in
[
Yas25
, Example 4.5]
.

So, let
\chi\colon S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S^{\prime}
be a Sarkisov link (

) between sextic del Pezzo surfaces at a point

of degree

. Let

(6.2)

Remark 6.3
.

The statements of Propositions
6.5
and
6.10
about new fields
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
can be also extracted from the proofs of
[

, Claim 5.8]
and
[

, Proposition 9.8]
. Our approach here is more naïve and is based on studying the Galois actions on
(-1)
-curves, and does not use either the motivic invariants of birational maps or mutations in derived categories

6.1.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
S
be a non-
\mathbf{k}
-rational sextic
G
-del Pezzo surface over
\mathbf{k}
and
\mathbf{F}
be the splitting field of its hexagon. Let
p
be a point of degree 2 on
S
with splitting field
\mathbf{E}
. Let
\chi:S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S^{\prime}
be a Sarkisov link based at
p
. Then
S^{\prime}
is a sextic del Pezzo surface over
\mathbf{k}
and one has
\mathbf{F}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E})^{H}
for the splitting field of its hexagon, where
H\subset\Gal(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
is a subgroup and one of the following cases holds.

(1)

If
\mathbf{E}\subset\mathbf{F}
, then
H=\{\mathrm{id}\}
and
\mathbf{F}^{\prime}=\mathbf{F}
. In particular,
\Gal(\mathbf{F}^{\prime}/\mathbf{k})=\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})
.

(2)

If
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{k}
, then
H=\langle h\rangle
. In particular,
\Gal(\mathbf{F}^{\prime}/\mathbf{k})\simeq(\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})/H)\times\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
.

Furthermore, one has
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}=\mathbf{L}
.

Remark 6.6
.

In the case where
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,f\rangle}
we have
\mathbf{F}^{\prime}=\mathbf{F}
, but the embedding changes (compare to Remark
3.6
) and therefore also the surface changes.

Proof.

The two mentioned cases are the only possible ones and are mutually exclusive. We consider them separately.

Case
\mathbf{E}\subset\mathbf{F}
:

Then
\mathbf{E}\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}
and the generators of
\Gal(\mathbf{E}\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})=\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})
belong to the set
\{f,g,h\}
. We now recover their actions on the hexagon
\Sigma^{\prime}
of
S^{\prime}
.

\bullet
:

First,
g
acts trivially on the components of
p
, hence fixes
E_{4}
and
E_{5}
on
T
. The action on
\Sigma^{\prime}
can be recovered either from intersection indices, or by using the identifications made in the beginning of this subsection. Namely, since
g
acts as
E_{1}\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.52 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.25pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.46 L 0 -3.46}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,E_{2}\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.52 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.25pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.46 L 0 -3.46}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,E_{3}\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.52 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.25pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.46 L 0 -3.46}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,E_{1}
, we find that
g(L_{14})
corresponds to the curve passing through
p_{4}
,
E_{2}
and
F_{2}
, i.e.
L_{24}
. We see that
g
acts by the clock-wise rotation by
2\pi/3
on
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

\bullet
:

Recall that
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})
cannot be
\langle g,f\rangle\simeq\Sym_{3}
since
\indexx(S)=2
. If
\langle g,h\rangle\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6
then the action of
h
on the components of
p
, hence on
E_{4}
and
E_{5}
, is non-trivial (since
p
is not
\mathbf{k}
-rational). As above, we find that
h
exchanges the opposite sides of
\Sigma^{\prime}
and conclude that all non-trivial elements of the Galois group act non-trivially on the new hexagon
\Sigma^{\prime}
, hence
H=\{\mathrm{id}\}
and
\mathbf{F}
is the splitting field of
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Now, if
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})=\langle g,f,h\rangle\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
, then we notice that the action of
f
on
\Sigma^{\prime}
is nontrivial, regardless of whether
f
permutes the components of
p
or not. Namely, either it acts as
L_{15}\leftrightarrow L_{14}
,
L_{34}\leftrightarrow L_{25}
,
L_{24}\leftrightarrow L_{35}
(when the components of
p
are permuted by
f
, i.e.
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,s\rangle}
), or as
L_{25}\leftrightarrow L_{35}
,
L_{34}\leftrightarrow L_{24}
(when the components of
p
are not permuted by
f
, i.e.
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,f\rangle}
). On the other hand, if
h
does not permute the components of
p
, then it acts trivially on
\Sigma^{\prime}
and hence
S^{\prime}
is a
\Sym_{3}
-del Pezzo surface, which is impossible as
\indexx(S^{\prime})=2
. Therefore,
h
permutes the components of
p
and hence acts by central symmetry on
\Sigma^{\prime}
, see Figures
3(a)
and
3(b)
. This again implies that the new action
\Psi
in (
6.2
) is faithful.

If
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})=\langle g,h\rangle\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6
, then
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}^{g}
. Since the action of
g
and
h
on
\Sigma^{\prime}
is the same as on
\Sigma
, we conclude that
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}={\mathbf{F}^{\prime}}^{g}=\mathbf{F}^{g}
. If
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})=\langle g,h,f\rangle\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
, then
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,f\rangle}
or
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,s\rangle}
as we know from Proposition
5.12
. In the former case,
g
and
f
preserve the components of
p
and hence we are in the situation of Figure
3(b)
. The contraction to Severi-Brauer surfaces is thus defined over
\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,f\rangle}
, i.e.
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=\mathbf{E}
. In the latter case,
g
preserves the components of
p
, while
f=s\circ h
does not, hence we are in the situation of Figure
3(a)
. The contraction to Severi-Brauer surfaces is thus defined over
\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,s\rangle}
, i.e. once again
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=\mathbf{E}
. Finally, it is clear that in all the cases
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}=\mathbf{F}^{h}=\mathbf{L}
.

Case
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{k}
:

Then
\Gal(\mathbf{E}\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})\times\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})
. Let
t
be the generator of
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
. Note that
g
,
h
and
f
act trivially on the components of
p
, while the automorphism
t
permutes the components of
p
and preserves the curves
E_{1},E_{2},E_{3},F_{1},F_{2},F_{3}
. Therefore,
g
and
f
act on
\Sigma^{\prime}
as shown on Figure
3(b)
,
h
acts trivially on
\Sigma^{\prime}
and
t
switches its opposite sides. Thus
H=\langle h\rangle
, and we have either
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E})^{\langle g,h\rangle}=\mathbf{E}
(if
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
), or
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E})^{\langle g,h,f\rangle}=\mathbf{E}
(if
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
). Finally, in all cases
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{E}\mathbf{F})^{\langle h,t\rangle}=\mathbf{F}^{h}=\mathbf{L}
.

∎

Corollary 6.7
.

Let
S
be a
G
-del Pezzo surface of degree 6 and index
2
with Severi-Brauer data
\{\mathbf{F},\ \mathbf{K},\ \mathbb{P}^{2}_{\mathbf{K}},\ \mathbf{L},\ Y\}
, and
\chi\colon S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S^{\prime}
be a Sarkisov link of type II based at a
2
-point with splitting field
\mathbf{E}
. Then the Severi-Brauer data of

is given by
\{\mathbf{F}^{\prime},\ \mathbf{E},\ \mathbb{P}^{2}_{\mathbf{E}},\ \mathbf{L},\ Y\}
. Moreover the surface
S^{\prime}
is uniquely determined by

and
\mathbf{E}
.

In this case, the surface

. We now consider the blow-up

Corollary 6.8
.

Consider the Sarkisov link (
6.1
) at a point of degree 3. The morphism
\eta
contracts
E_{4}
,
E_{5}
and
E_{6}
. The morphism
\eta^{\prime}
contracts
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
and
C_{3}
. The hexagon
\Sigma^{\prime}
consists of (the
\eta^{\prime}
-images of) the curves
L_{45}
,
L_{46}
,
L_{56}
,
C_{4}
,
C_{5}
,
C_{6}
, whose configuration is shown on Figure
5
.

Remark 6.9
.

The configuration of
(-1)
-curves on the cubic surface
T
, appearing in our link, is shown on Figure
4
. To visualize this graph, we adapt Coxeter’s approach
[
Cox40
]
, where the initial double-six
[
Cox40
, p. 457]
is chosen as

E_{1}

E_{2}

E_{3}

E_{4}

E_{5}

E_{6}

C_{1}

C_{2}

C_{3}

C_{4}

C_{5}

C_{6}

Two vertices are adjacent in the Schläfli graph if and only if the corresponding pair of
(-1)
-curves on
T
are skew. The adjacencies should be understood as follows. Consider a vertex
x
on the outer circle and all the vertices along the corresponding axis in the graph. If there are 4 such vertices, then
x
is connected to the two vertices on the inner circle that lie on this axis. If there are 3 such vertices (i.e., the axis passes through the center), then
x
is connected to two (out of the three coinciding) vertices at the center. Now consider a vertex
y
on the inner circle and all the vertices along the corresponding axis in the graph. If there are 4 such vertices, then
y
is connected to the two vertices on the outer circle that lie on this axis. If there are 3 such vertices (i.e., the axis passes through the center), then
y
is connected to exactly two vertices in the centre.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
S
be a non-
\mathbf{k}
-rational sextic
G
-del Pezzo surface over
\mathbf{k}
and
\mathbf{F}
be the splitting field of its hexagon. Let
p
be a point of degree 3 on
S
with splitting field
\mathbf{E}
. Let
\chi:S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S^{\prime}
be a Sarkisov link based at
p
. Then
S^{\prime}
is a sextic del Pezzo surface over
\mathbf{k}
and one has
\mathbf{F}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E})^{H}
for the splitting field of its hexagon, where
H\subset\Gal(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
is a subgroup and one of the following cases holds.

(1)

\mathbf{E}\subset\mathbf{F}
. Then
H=\{\mathrm{id}\}
, i.e.
\mathbf{F}^{\prime}=\mathbf{F}
.

(2)

[\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}:\mathbf{k}]=2
. Then
H=\langle g\rangle
if
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
or
G\simeq\Sym_{3}
, and
H=\langle g\rangle
or
H=\langle g,s\rangle
if
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}

(
the exact answer depends on the extension
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k}
, see the proof for the details
)
.

(3)

\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{k}
. Then
H=\langle g\rangle
if
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
or
G\simeq\Sym_{3}
, and
H=\langle g,s\rangle
if
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
.

Furthermore, one has
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=\mathbf{K}
and
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}=\mathbf{E}
, whenever
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
is defined. If
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
is not defined, i.e.
\Gal(\mathbf{F}^{\prime}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\Sym_{3}
, then
\mathbf{F}^{\prime}=\mathbf{E}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k}
is of degree 3 or 6, with
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
or
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\Sym_{3}
respectively, one has

\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{E}
, or

\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{k}
, or

\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}
is quadratic over
\mathbf{k}
, or

\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}\neq\mathbf{E}
is cubic over
\mathbf{k}
. However this last case is not possible, as
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}
is then not Galois, which contradicts Remark
2.3
. So, we deal with the three remaining cases.

Case
\mathbf{E}\subset\mathbf{F}
:

Then
\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}
and the generators of
\Gal(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})=\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})
belong to the set
\{f,g,h\}
. If
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
then
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}^{h}
and
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})=\langle g\rangle
; if
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\Sym_{3}
then
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}
and
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})=\langle g,f\rangle
, as otherwise
\mathbf{E}
is not Galois over
\mathbf{k}
; if
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
then
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}^{h}
and
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})=\langle g,f\rangle
. In what follows, we assume, without loss of generality, that
g
acts by
p_{4}\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.52 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.25pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.46 L 0 -3.46}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,p_{5}\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.52 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.25pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.46 L 0 -3.46}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,p_{6}\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.52 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.25pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.46 L 0 -3.46}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,p_{4}
, while
f
fixes
p_{4}
and swaps
p_{5}
and
p_{6}
. It is then straightforward, by using the identifications made in beginning of Section
6.2
, to recover the action of Galois elements on
\Sigma^{\prime}
. For example, since
g
preserves the set
\{E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}\}
and permutes the points
p_{4}
,
p_{5}
,
p_{6}
as we said above, one has
g(C_{4})=C_{5}
.

Suppose that
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
or
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
, so
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}^{h}
. Then
h
fixes
p_{4}
,
p_{5}
,
p_{6}
. Since
h
switches
E_{i}
with
F_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, we find that
h(C_{4})=L_{56}
; similarly,
h(C_{5})=L_{46}
and
h(C_{6})=L_{45}
. Similarly, by recalling the action of
f
on
\Sigma
, we deduce that
f
switches
C_{5}
with
L_{45}
,
C_{6}
with
L_{46}
and
C_{4}
with
L_{56}
. To sum up, the action of
g
,
h
and
f
on
\Sigma^{\prime}
is shown on Figure
5(a)
. In particular,
H=\{\mathrm{id}\}
, and
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=\mathbf{K}
,
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}=\mathbf{E}
when
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
or
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
, and
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=\mathbf{K}
when
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\Sym_{3}
.

Case
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}
is quadratic over
\mathbf{k}
:

Note that
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\Sym_{3}
in this case. We use Remark
2.3
with
\mathbf{E}_{1}=\mathbf{F}
,
\mathbf{E}_{2}=\mathbf{E}
to calculate the Galois group
\Gal(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
. As in that Remark, we denote by
t
and
w
some elements of order
2
and
3
, respectively, in
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
.

If
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
or
G\simeq\Sym_{3}
, then
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}^{g}=\mathbf{E}^{w}
. If
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
, then
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,u\rangle}=\mathbf{E}^{w}
for
u\in\{s,f,h\}
. Set
t^{\prime}=h
for
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
, set
t^{\prime}=f
for
G\simeq\Sym_{3}
, and choose the element
t^{\prime}\in\mathrm{D}_{6}
as in the Remark
2.3
when
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
. In all three cases, observe that

t^{\prime}|_{\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}}=t|_{\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}}

is the unique non-trivial element of
\Gal(\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})
. Thus
\Gal(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
is generated by [the preimages under the isomorphism given in the Remark of]
(\mathrm{id},w)
,
(t^{\prime},t)
,
(g,\mathrm{id})
, and in the case of
\mathrm{D}_{6}
we also add
(u,\mathrm{id})
. By a slight abuse of notation, we will denote those elements by
w,t,g,u
, respectively.

Notice that
g
and
u
act trivially on the components of the point
p
(since, by Remark
2.3
, their restrictions on
\mathbf{E}
give trivial automorphisms), while
w
acts as a
3
-cycle on its geometric components and
t
fixes one component and switches the other two components.

Since
g
fixes all components of
p
and does not exchange the two triplets
\{E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}\}
and
\{F_{1},F_{2},F_{3}\}
in
\Sigma
, its action on
\Sigma^{\prime}
is trivial. The action of
w
is always by rotation of order 3. The action of
t
on
\Sigma^{\prime}
is by
t(C_{4})=L_{56}
,
t(C_{5})=L_{45}
,
t(C_{6})=L_{46}
. Thus, for
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
and
G\simeq\Sym_{3}
we have
H=\langle g\rangle
and therefore
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E}^{\langle g,w\rangle}=\mathbf{F}^{g}=\mathbf{K}
. In both cases, one has
\Gal(\mathbf{F}^{\prime}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\Sym_{3}
. Note that
\mathbf{F}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E})^{g}=\mathbf{E}
.

Now suppose
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
. If
u=(h,\mathrm{id})
or
u=(f,\mathrm{id})
then
u
acts as central symmetry on
\Sigma^{\prime}
and thus
H=\langle g\rangle
.
We observe that
\Gal(\mathbf{F}^{\prime}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
and therefore
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E})^{\langle g,tu,w\rangle}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,s\rangle}=\mathbf{K}
and
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E})^{\langle g,u\rangle}=\mathbf{E}
.

In the case
u=(s,\mathrm{id})
we see that
s
fixes the components of
p
and does not exchange the two triplets
\{E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}\}
and
\{F_{1},F_{2},F_{3}\}
, hence it acts trivially on
\Sigma^{\prime}
and
H=\langle g,s\rangle
. Now we have
\Gal(\mathbf{F}^{\prime}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\Sym_{3}
and therefore we have that
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E})^{\langle g,s,w\rangle}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,s\rangle}=\mathbf{K}
. Note that
\mathbf{F}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E})^{\langle g,s\rangle}=\mathbf{E}
. To sum up, possible transitions between Galois groups are presented in Table
1
.

\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}=\langle g,h\rangle

\Sym_{3}=\langle g,f\rangle

\mathrm{D}_{6}=\langle g,h,f\rangle

\Sym_{3}=\langle w,t\rangle

\Sym_{3}

\Sym_{3}

\mathrm{D}_{6}
or
\Sym_{3}

Table 1
.
Each cell indicates a group isomorphic to
\Gal(\mathbf{F}^{\prime}/\mathbf{k})
.

Case
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{k}
:

Then
\Gal(\mathbf{E}\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})\times\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})
. As before,
w
and
t
are the generators of order
3
and
2
in
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})
, or take just
w
if
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
. We will assume that
w(p_{4})=p_{5}
,
w(p_{5})=p_{6}
, while
t
fixes
p_{4}
and swaps
p_{5}
with
p_{6}
. Note that
g
,
h
and
f
act trivially on the components of
p
. So,
g(C_{4})=C_{4}
and we find that
g
acts trivially on
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Further,
h
and
f
both act by swapping the opposite sides of
\Sigma^{\prime}
, so in particular
s=h\circ f
acts trivially on
\Sigma^{\prime}
. One has
w(C_{4})=C_{5}
,
w(C_{5})=C_{6}
, while
t(C_{4})=C_{4}
,
t(L_{45})=L_{46}
,
t(C_{5})=C_{6}
. To sum up, the actions are shown on Figure
5(b)
, and the corresponding Galois groups are summarized in Table
2
.

\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}=\langle g,h\rangle

\Sym_{3}=\langle g,f\rangle

\mathrm{D}_{6}=\langle g,h,f\rangle

\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}=\langle w\rangle

\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}=\langle w,h\rangle

\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}=\langle w,f\rangle

\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}=\langle w,f\rangle

\Sym_{3}=\langle w,t\rangle

\mathrm{D}_{6}=\langle w,t,h\rangle

\mathrm{D}_{6}=\langle w,t,f\rangle

\mathrm{D}_{6}=\langle w,t,f\rangle

Table 2
.
Each cell contains a new action of the Galois group on
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Notice that we have
H=\langle g\rangle
if
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
or
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\Sym_{3}
, and
H=\langle g,s\rangle
if
\Gal(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
. In each case, we see directly that
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=\mathbf{K}
and
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}=\mathbf{E}
.
∎

Corollary 6.11
.

Let
S
be a
G
-del Pezzo surface of degree 6 and index
3
with Severi-Brauer data
\{\mathbf{F},\ \mathbf{K},\ X,\ \mathbf{L},\ \mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{L}}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{L}}\}
and
\chi\colon S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S^{\prime}
be a Sarkisov link of type II based at a
3
-point with splitting field
\mathbf{E}
. Then the Severi-Brauer data of
S^{\prime}
is given by
\{\mathbf{F}^{\prime},\ \mathbf{K},\ X,\ \mathbf{E},\ \mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}\}
. Moreover, the surface
S^{\prime}
is uniquely determined by
S
and
\mathbf{E}
.

6.3.
Birational rigidity of sextic del Pezzo surfaces

Now we will derive several corollaries from the description of Sarkisov links between sextic del Pezzo surfaces. At the end of the paragraph, we will prove Theorem
C
.

Given a sextic
G
-del Pezzo surface
S
and an integer
d\in\{2,3\}
, put

\Fields_{d}(S)=\big\{\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k}\colon\mathbf{E}\ \text{is a splitting field of a $d$-point on $S$ in general position}\big\}.

It turns out that these sets are birational invariants of a minimal sextic del Pezzo surface
S
.

Proposition 6.12
.

If
S
and
S^{\prime}
are two birational non-
\mathbf{k}
-rational sextic del Pezzo surfaces of Picard rank 1, then
\Fields_{d}(S)=\Fields_{d}(S^{\prime})
for
d\in\{2,3\}
.

Proof.

It is enough to show that
\Fields_{d}(S)\subseteq\Fields_{d}(S^{\prime})
. Furthermore, it is enough to show that this inclusion holds for
S
and
S^{\prime}
related by a single Sarkisov link
\chi\colon S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S^{\prime}
at a point of degree
d
(recall that Geiser birational involutions, which may appear in the index 2 case, lead to an isomorphic surface). So, let
\mathbf{E}\in\Fields_{d}(S)
and let
p\in S
be a
d
-point in general position with splitting field
\mathbf{E}
. We consider
\indexx(S)=2
and
\indexx(S)=3
cases separately.

Assume that
d=2
. If
p\not\in\Exc(\chi)
then
7
7

7

Here and below
\Exc(\phi)
denotes the
exceptional set
of a birational map
\phi\colon X\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,Y
between surfaces
X
and
Y
. Recall that there exists a unique maximal pair of open subsets
U\subset X
,
V\subset Y
such that
\phi
restricts as an isomorphism
U\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}}V
. Then
\Exc(\phi)=X\setminus U
. By Zariski’s theorem, an irreducible curve
C\subset X
is exceptional if and only if
\phi(C)
is a point. In particular, the exceptional set of the link (
2.3
) consists of
\eta
-images of curves contracted by
\eta^{\prime}
.

\chi
is a local
\mathbf{k}
-isomorphism at
p
and
\chi(p)\in S^{\prime}
has
\mathbf{E}
as its splitting field. Suppose that
p
is contained in
\Exc(\chi)\setminus\Ind(\chi)
. In the notation of Section
6.1
, one has
\Exc(\chi)=\eta(C)\cup\eta(L_{45})
and
\Ind(\chi)=\eta(C)\cap\eta(L_{45})
. Then
\chi(p)\subset\Ind(\chi^{-1})
. Moreover, since
\chi(p)
is
\Gal(\overline{\mathbf{k}}/\mathbf{k})
-invariant,
\Ind(\chi^{-1})
has two geometric components and
S^{\prime}(\mathbf{k})=\varnothing
, we have
\chi(p)=\Ind(\chi^{-1})
. Since
\chi
is defined over
\mathbf{k}
, the 2-point
\Ind(\chi^{-1})
splits over
\mathbf{E}
and there is no smaller field over which it splits. Notice that
\Ind(\chi^{-1})
is in general position on
S^{\prime}
by definition of a Sarkisov link, thus
\mathbf{E}\in\Fields_{2}(S^{\prime})
. It remains to consider the case
p=\Ind(\chi)
. By Proposition
6.5
, the field
\mathbf{E}
is exactly the one over which
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
-contractions to Severi-Brauer surfaces are defined. By Proposition
5.15
, there are
2
-points in general position that split over
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=\mathbf{E}
on
S
.

Assume that
d=3
. If
p\not\in\Exc(\chi)
, then again
\chi
is a local
\mathbf{k}
-isomorphism at
p
and
\chi(p)\in S^{\prime}
has
\mathbf{E}
as its splitting field. Suppose that
p\in\Exc(\chi)\setminus\Ind(\chi)
. Notice that
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
or
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\Sym_{3}
. As above we observe that
\chi(p)=\Ind(\chi^{-1})
since
S^{\prime}
has no points of degree 1 or 2, and therefore
\Ind(\chi^{-1})
splits over
\mathbf{E}
. Since in the
\Gal(\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\Sym_{3}
case there is no Galois extention of degree
3
in between
\mathbf{k}\subset\mathbf{E}
we conclude that
\mathbf{E}
is the splitting field of the 3-point
\Ind(\chi^{-1})
, which is in general position on
S^{\prime}
by the definition of a Sarkisov link. It remains to consider the case
p=\Ind(\chi)
. But then we finish as in the case
d=2
. Indeed, either
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
is the field over which the contractions to involution surfaces are defined, or
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}^{\prime}
(when
\Gal(\mathbf{F}^{\prime}/\mathbf{k})\simeq\Sym_{3}
). In both cases, Proposition
5.14
gives the existence of 3-points in general position on
S^{\prime}
with splitting field
\mathbf{E}
.
∎

Next, we investigate birational rigidity of sextic
G
-del Pezzo surfaces (see Definition
1.2
).

Theorem 6.13
.

Let
S
be a
G
-del Pezzo surface of degree 6 over a perfect field
\mathbf{k}
. If
\indexx(S)=1
, then
S
is
\mathbf{k}
-rational and hence is not birationally rigid. If
\indexx(S)=6
, then
S
is birationally super-rigid. In the remaining two cases, the following holds.

(1)

If
\indexx(S)=2
, then
S
is birationally rigid if and only if
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
and every 2-point on
S
splits over
\mathbf{K}
.

(2)

If
\indexx(S)=3
, then
S
is birationally rigid if and only if either
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
or
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
and every 3-point on
S
splits over
\mathbf{L}
, or
G\simeq\Sym_{3}
and every 3-point on
S
splits over
\mathbf{F}
.

Proof.

The first two statements were proven in Section
5.1
. Note that
S
is birationally rigid if and only if every Sarkisov link on
S
leads to an isomorphic surface. Let
\chi\colon S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S^{\prime}
be a Sarkisov link. We keep the usual notation for the Severi-Brauer data of these surfaces. By the results of Section
3
, we need to find when the data of
S^{\prime}
is equivalent to the one of
S
.

If
\indexx(S)=2
, then Proposition
6.5
says that
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}=\mathbf{L}
and
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=\mathbf{K}
(note that both fields are defined as
G
is not isomorphic to
\Sym_{3}
). Therefore
S^{\prime}\simeq S
if and only if
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
and every 2-point on
S
splits over
\mathbf{K}
, because for
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
we can always find 2-points split over
\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,f\rangle}
by Proposition
5.15
. In the
\indexx(S)=3
case, we conclude by Proposition
6.10
.
∎

Finally, we can prove Theorem
C
.

Proof of Theorem
C
.

Let
S
be a solid del Pezzo surface; recall that we assume
\Pic(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. If
K_{S}^{2}=9
, then
S(\mathbf{k})=\varnothing
, as otherwise the blow-up of a
\mathbf{k}
-rational point maps
S
to a conic bundle
\mathbb{F}_{1}\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{k}}^{1}
. So,
S
is a non-trivial Severi-Brauer surface and one has
\Pl(S)=2
by Proposition
2.6
. Suppose that
K_{S}^{2}=8
. If
S(\mathbf{k})\neq\varnothing
, then
S
is
\mathbf{k}
-rational and hence is not solid. If
S(\mathbf{k})=\varnothing
, then
[
Tre23
, Theorem 1.6]
implies that
S
is birationally rigid. If
K_{S}^{2}=7
or
K_{S}^{2}=5
, then it is well known (see e.g.
[
SD72
]
) that
S(\mathbf{k})\neq\varnothing
and such surfaces are
\mathbf{k}
-rational by Theorem
5.3
, hence they are not solid. Let
K_{S}^{2}=4
. If
S(\mathbf{k})\neq\varnothing
, then the blow-up of a
\mathbf{k}
-rational point leads to a cubic surface with a structure of a conic bundle, given by the residue conics passing through the exceptional divisor; thus
S
is not solid. Otherwise, the classification of Sarkisov links
[
Isk96
, Theorem 2.6]
shows that
S
is birationally rigid. The same classification shows that del Pezzo surfaces of degree
\leqslant 3
are birationally rigid. Now the result follows from Example
5.16
.
∎

7.
Quotients of groups of birational self-maps

7.1.
The associated graph

We will consider the following standard equivalence relation between Sarkisov links:

.

Definition 7.3
.

Let
S
be a sextic del Pezzo surface with
\rk\Pic(S)=1
. We define the directed graph
\Graph_{S}
associated to
S
as follows:

\bullet

The vertices of
\Graph_{S}
are all sextic del Pezzo surfaces of Picard rank one which are birational to
S
, considered up to
\mathbf{k}
-isomorphism.

\bullet

The edges are Sarkisov links between two surfaces, considered up to the equivalence.

Proposition 7.4
.

.
Every two vertices of
\Graph_{S}
are connected by at least one edge.

Proof.

Pick any two vertices in
\Graph_{S}
, and suppose they correspond to the surfaces
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
. Since
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
are both birational to
S
, the Sarkisov program (Theorem
2.15
) implies that they are connected by a sequence of links

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z=S_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{r+1}=Z^{\prime},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{r-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{r}}

where each
\chi_{i}
starts at a point
p_{i}\in S_{i}
with splitting field
\mathbf{E}_{i}
. We can assume that all
p_{i}
are
d
-points with
d\in\{2,3\}
, since birational Geiser involutions lead to an isomorphic surface (recall that there are no points of degree 1 or 5, as we assume
S
irrational over
\mathbf{k}
). We may assume that
\indexx(S)\in\{2,3\}
, since otherwise
S
is birationally super-rigid and
\Graph_{S}
is a singleton. Suppose that
\indexx(S)=2
. Then we can assume that all
p_{i}
are 2-points.
By Proposition
6.12
, there is a
2
-point
p\in Z
in general position with splitting field
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{E}_{r}
. So, consider the Sarkisov link
\chi\colon Z\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,Z^{\prime\prime}
centred at this point. We claim that
Z^{\prime}\simeq Z^{\prime\prime}
, which will finish the proof in this case. Let
\{\mathbf{K},\mathbf{L}\}
,
\{\mathbf{K}^{\prime},\mathbf{L}^{\prime}\}
and
\{\mathbf{K}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{L}^{\prime\prime}\}
be the fields from the Severi-Brauer data for
Z
,
Z^{\prime}
and
Z^{\prime\prime}
, respectively; note that all these fields are defined, as we are in the index 2 case. Then
\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{L}^{\prime}=\mathbf{L}^{\prime\prime}
by Proposition
6.5
, and by the same Proposition
\mathbf{K}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
. Since the splitting fields of
Z^{\prime}
and
Z^{\prime\prime}
are
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{K}^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{L}^{\prime\prime}
, respectively, we are done.

In the
\indexx(Z)=3
case we can use a similar argument if neither
Z
, nor
Z^{\prime}
is an
\Sym_{3}
-surface. Now assume that one among
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
is an
\Sym_{3}
-surface. By possibly inverting the path of Sarkisov links, we can assume that
Z^{\prime}
is a
\Sym_{3}
-surface. If
\mathbf{E}
is the splitting field of
p_{r}
, then
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}^{\prime}
is the splitting field of the hexagon on
Z^{\prime}
by Proposition
6.10
. Thus there is a
3
-point
p\in Z
in general position with splitting field
\mathbf{E}
by Proposition
6.12
. Therefore we can find a Sarkisov link
\chi:Z\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,Z^{\prime\prime}
based at
p
. Let
\mathbf{K}
,
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
, and
\mathbf{K}^{\prime\prime}
be the respective fields from the Severi-Brauer data. Proposition
6.10
implies that
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=\mathbf{K}^{\prime\prime}
. Notice that
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{K}^{\prime}\subset\mathbf{F}^{\prime}=\mathbf{E}
then, so in particular
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}\neq\mathbf{k}
. Thus either
\mathbf{E}\subset\mathbf{F}
or
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}
is quadratic over
\mathbf{k}
. The first case is clearly impossible for
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
and is not possible either for
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
, since in that case no
3
-point splits over an
\Sym_{3}
-subfield that contains
\mathbf{K}
(see Proposition
5.12
). Thus
G\simeq\Sym_{3}
,
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}
and we conclude that
Z\simeq Z^{\prime}
by Theorem
3.14
. So, we assume that
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}
is quadratic over
\mathbf{k}
. If
G\simeq\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
or
G\simeq\Sym_{3}
, then
\mathbf{F}^{\prime\prime}=(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E})^{g}=\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}^{\prime}
(see the proof of Proposition
6.10
), hence
Z^{\prime}\simeq Z^{\prime\prime}
by Theorem
3.14
. If
G\simeq\mathrm{D}_{6}
, then
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,s\rangle}
, hence
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}^{\langle g,s\rangle}
and again
\mathbf{F}^{\prime\prime}=(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{E})^{\langle g,s\rangle}=\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}^{\prime}
by Proposition
6.10
. We conclude that
Z^{\prime\prime}\simeq Z^{\prime}
.
∎

At this point, we are able to prove Theorem
B
.

Proof of Theorem
B
.

By the results of Section
5.1
, the described cases cover all possibilities and correspond to indices 1, 2, 3, and 6, respectively. The birationality in the case of index
1
is clear, since all
\mathbf{k}
-rational surfaces are birational to each other. The birationality criterium in the index
6
case follows from Proposition
5.8
and Theorem
A
. Otherwise, we conclude by Corollaries
6.7
,
6.11
and Proposition
7.4
.
∎

Notation 7.5
.

Consider the graph
\Graph_{S}
, and let
(V,E)
be the sets of its vertices and edges, respectively. Choose for every vertex
v\in V
(i.e. an isomorphism class of a surface) in
\mathcal{G}_{S}
a representative
S_{v}
, and put
\mathcal{R}_{V}=\{S_{v}\}_{v\in V}
. Further, choose for every
S_{v}\in\mathcal{R}_{V}\setminus\{S\}
one Sarkisov link
\chi_{v}:S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S_{v}
and collect those in the set
\mathcal{R}_{E}=\{\chi_{v}\colon S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S_{v}\}_{v\in V}
.

In what follows, by a
tour
in a graph we mean a closed walk: a sequence of adjacent edges that starts and ends at the same vertex, possibly repeating vertices and edges. A (generalized)
cycle
for us will be a closed walk in which all vertices (and hence edges) are distinct, except for the first and last vertex, and we also allow loops, i.e. edges that connect a vertex to itself.

. We take a link
\chi:S\,\hbox to14.73pt{\vbox to0.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-0.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 16.71 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.07642pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.97 0 L -1.78 2.38 L 0 0 L -1.78 -2.38}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,S_{j-1}
and insert
\chi\circ\chi^{-1}
into the sequence. Thus we get a cycle based at
S
in the path. We now consider the tour starting from
\chi
and iterate the process, eventually getting the desired decomposition. An example is shown on Figure

.
